INTEGRATED REVIEW

1. Background
The Applicant resubmitted the biologics license application (BLA) for Isembyld (SRK-015,
apitegromab), following a Complete Response (CR) action issued on September 22, 2025.
Apitegromab is a fully human immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4) monoclonal antibody that specifically
binds to inactive precursor forms of myostatin and is indicated for the treatment of adult and
pediatric patients with spinal muscular atrophy (SMA) who are currently receiving a survival
motor neuron 2 (SMN2)-targeted treatment.
Spinal muscular atrophy (SMA) is a rare, inherited neuromuscular disease caused by mutations
or deletions in the SMN1 (survival motor neuron 1) gene on chromosome 5q, leading to a
deficiency of SMN protein. A second gene, SMN2, produces a small amount of functional SMN
protein, and the number of SMN2 copies a patient has generally correlates with disease
severity. Without adequate SMN protein, lower motor neurons progressively degenerate,
resulting in muscle weakness and atrophy that primarily affects proximal muscles involved in
breathing, swallowing, and limb movement. When SMA presents before six months of age,, it
leads to death or permanent ventilator dependence before age two years, without an FDAapproved intervention. There are three FDA-approved therapies for treatment of SMA.
Nusinersen (Spinraza) and risdiplam (Evrysdi) are SMN2-targeted therapies, and onasemnogene
abeparvovec-xioi (Zolgensma) is a single-dose gene-replacement therapy that delivers a
functional copy of the SMN1 gene. Although there are available therapies, there remains an
unmet need for safe and effective therapies for SMA.
In the original BLA review, substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with one
adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence. In the original
BLA, the Applicant had submitted results from a single, positive, adequate, well-controlled
study (SAPPHIRE) that won on a clinically relevant primary endpoint with favorable trends on
the secondary endpoints. Additionally, confirmatory evidence came from pharmacodynamic/
mechanistic data on latent myostatin, a target engagement biomarker. Overall, there were no
major safety concerns that would preclude approval. Findings in the single adequate and wellcontrolled study indicate potential concerns for increased risk of fractures, which should be
described in the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling, and risk of elevated creatine
phosphokinase (CPK), both of which can be mitigated through patient labeling. Enhanced
pharmacovigilance will also be requested to further characterize and follow the risk for bone
fractures.
The deficiencies that led to the original CR action were related to deficiencies identified during
(b) (4)
a surveillance inspection of the

manufacturing facility.
No new efficacy data were included in this resubmission; however, the Applicant did provide
updated safety data from an ongoing open-label safety study and an expanded access study.

The overall control strategy incorporates control over raw materials, facilities and
equipment, the manufacturing process, adventitious agents, microbial contamination, and
release and stability of the DS and DP. The manufacturing processes and overall control
strategies for Isembyld (apitegromab-mstn) as described in the license are appropriately
established to ensure consistency and quality of the final product; therefore, lot variability is
not a concern. The assays used for the immunogenicity assessment in the clinical studies to
support this BLA are adequately validated and suitable for their intended purpose.
OPQ recommendation: Approval.

5. Clinical/Statistical-Efficacy
Dr. Monica Petluru was the clinical reviewer for this application.
No new efficacy data were included in this resubmission. See the Integrated Review of the
original BLA submission dated September 22, 2025, for a detailed review of the data supporting
substantial evidence of effectiveness. The following discussion is primarily extracted from the
September 22, 2025, integrated review of the original BLA submission.
In the original submission, the Applicant provided efficacy results from a single adequate and
well-controlled study, SRK-015-003 (SAPPHIRE), and confirmatory evidence (CE) of biomarker
and clinical data.
SAPPHIRE was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter study in 188
subjects with SMA ages 2 years and older who were nonambulatory and on stable background
therapy with either of the currently approved SMN2 targeting therapies (nusinersen or
risdiplam). Subjects received intravenous (IV) apitegromab doses of 10 mg/kg or 20 mg/kg
every 4 weeks or placebo. The primary endpoint was the mean change from baseline to 12
months in the Hammersmith Functional Motor Scale Expanded (HFMSE). The secondary
endpoints were change from baseline to 12 months in the Revised Upper Limb Module (RULM)
and in the World Health Organization (WHO) Motor Development Milestones, and the
proportion of subjects with a ≥3-point change from baseline in HFMSE at 12 months.
The primary endpoint was the change from baseline in the HFMSE at 12 months analyzed in the
pooled 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose groups compared to placebo and in the 20
mg/kg apitegromab dose group compared to placebo; analyses were controlled for multiple
comparisons using the Hochberg method. These analyses were performed in the subgroup of
subjects who were 2 to 12 years of age and had at least one postbaseline assessment on the
HFMSE.
In the analysis of the pooled 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose groups, there was a
statistically significant difference of 1.8 points in the HFMSE change from baseline to Month 12
favoring apitegromab (0.6-point least squares [LS] mean in the pooled apitegromab group
versus -1.2-point LS mean in the placebo group; p=0.0192).
4
Reference ID: 5867963

In the analysis of the 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose group, there was a numerical difference of
1.4 points in the HFMSE change from baseline to Month 12 favoring apitegromab that was not
statistically significant (p=0.1149). Therefore, all other endpoints were considered exploratory.
However, a prespecified secondary endpoint, the HFMSE change from baseline at 12 months in
the 10 mg/kg apitegromab dose group, was nominally statistically significant, with a LS mean
difference in of 2.2 points favoring apitegromab compared to placebo (nominal p =0.0121).
The key secondary endpoints of RULM and WHO Motor Milestones showed numerical trends in
favor of apitegromab that did not achieve nominal statistical significance for either the pooled
or individual 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg dose groups. The proportion of clinical responders
(defined as improvement in the HFMSE ≥3 points by Month 12) was numerically higher in the
apitegromab groups compared to those in the placebo group and met nominal statistical
significance in the combined dose group and in the 10 mg/kg dose group, but not in the 20
mg/kg dose group.
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology evaluated the effect of dose on the primary endpoint in the
main and exploratory efficacy populations in SAPPHIRE. The greater observed treatment
response on the primary endpoint for 10 mg/kg compared to 20 mg/kg was considered to be
due to an imbalance between groups in the number of very young subjects 2 to 3 years of age,
whose responses were more variable and had a larger effect size than subjects 4 to 12 years of
age. When all subjects 2 to 3 years of age are excluded from the primary analysis, the
treatment difference for the 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg groups are more similar.
CE was provided from relevant pharmacodynamic and mechanistic data of latent myostatin
concentrations. The longitudinal changes (relative to baseline) in serum latent myostatin
concentrations were comparable between 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg in subjects 2 to 12 years old,
and there is no apparent dose-response in serum latent myostatin levels at the end of Year 1,
demonstrating that there is no significant difference between the 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg dose.
In subjects 13 to 21 years, 20 mg/kg apitegromab showed consistent trends in changes in the
latent myostatin levels in serum as was noted in both of the dose arms in 2- to 12-year-olds.
These pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data contributed to the confirmatory evidence of a
treatment benefit and that the drug is having its intended effect on the muscle.

6. Safety
Dr. Petluru conducted the safety review of this resubmission.
The primary safety analysis was based on review of SAPPHIRE, a 52-week, multicenter,
randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial in 128 subjects with SMA who were 2 to 21
years of age. Additional safety data came from ONYX, an open-label long-term safety study. For
details of the safety analysis of the original submission, see the Integrated Review of the
original BLA submission dated September 22, 2025.

5
Reference ID: 5867963

The safety database for apitegromab was adequate for the intended population and proposed
dosing regimen. The safety database was characterized in SAPPHIRE, a 52-week randomized,
double-blind, placebo-controlled, multinational phase 3 study, as well as with an open-label
study, ONYX. In subjects who received apitegromab 10 mg/kg in the main efficacy population of
SAPPHIRE, the most frequently reported (≥5% frequency and ≥5% more frequently than in
placebo) treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) vs placebo were upper respiratory tract
infections, vomiting, cough, other viral infections, headache, gastroenteritis, pharyngitis, rash,
diarrhea, injection site reactions, abdominal pain, arthralgia, contusion, fall, fractures, fatigue,
pain in extremity, hematoma, and myalgia. The SAEs that occurred in more than one subject
include pneumonia, dehydration, and acute respiratory failure. There were no adverse events
that led to treatment discontinuation in SAPPHIRE. There were no deaths in either SAPPHIRE or
ONYX.
In the original review, safety signals identified during SAPPHIRE included increased risk for bone
fractures and elevated creatine phosphate kinase levels. These risks can be adequately
addressed through labeling and through routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance. Warnings
and Precautions statements will include the risk of fractures. In addition, enhanced
pharmacovigilance will be recommended for bone fractures. A pregnancy outcomes study will
be a postmarketing requirement.
The Applicant submitted additional safety data from ONYX with a data cutoff date of August 29,
2025, as well as additional safety information for AEs and SAEs from ONYX, the ongoing EAP,
and limited information from the ongoing study OPAL (children <2 years of age with SMA). The
cutoff date for the previous 90-day safety update was January 10, 2025.
Per Dr. Petluru’s review of the safety data included in the resubmission, there were no deaths
(b) (6)
in ONYX or the EAP. One death occurred in a subject in OPAL following the data cutoff (a
-old who developed the fatal SAEs of respiratory syncytial virus bronchiolitis and acute
respiratory distress syndrome). SAEs reported in at least 2 subjects each in the resubmission
included scoliosis, pneumonia, acute respiratory failure, influenza, femur fracture, joint
dislocation, and gastroenteritis. Overall, 14 subjects experienced fractures during the reporting
period, 3 of whom had previously experienced fractures during either ONYX or SAPPHIRE that
were reported in the original BLA submission.
Some subjects in ONYX had abnormal CK values during the reporting period for the
resubmission, but these results were consistent with what was seen in the original BLA
submission. No clinically significant abnormalities in kidney function laboratory analyses were
reported. Two subjects had significant AST or ALT elevations in the resubmission, but a causal
association with apitegromab was not clear as the subjects had improvement in the
transaminases while on apitegromab and/or confounding factors.
Dr. Petluru concluded that no new clinical safety concerns or signals have been identified in the
additional safety information provided in the BLA resubmission, and the overall safety profile
has not changed from the original integrated review of BLA 761463. Additionally, given that
6
Reference ID: 5867963

fracture risk continues to be significant, enhanced pharmacovigilance for serious and
nonserious fracture events will be recommended in patients with SMA treated with
apitegromab, as previously discussed in the original integrated review for BLA 761463.

7. Advisory Committee Meeting
An advisory committee meeting was not deemed necessary because the application did not
raise significant public health questions on the role of the drug in the treatment of the disease
and there were no controversial issues that would benefit from advisory committee discussion;
therefore, outside expertise was not necessary.

8. Pediatrics
The submission included pediatric data from subjects 2 to 18 years of age.
Because this drug product for this indication has an orphan drug designation, the Applicant is
exempt from PREA requirements.

9. Labeling and Consumer Study Review
Labeling has been agreed upon. See Dr. Petluru’s review dated September 11, 2026, for a
detailed discussion of the edits to the labeling. The primary safety risk that will be mitigated in
the labeling is a Warning and Precaution for fractures:
ISEMBYLD may increase the risk of fractures, including serious fractures [see Adverse
Reactions (6.1)]. In Study 1 [see Clinical Studies (14)], 5/53 (9%) of patients treated with
ISEMBYLD 10 mg/kg and 3/53 (6%) of patients treated with ISEMBYLD 20 mg/kg (twice
the recommended dosage) experienced fractures compared with one patient in the placebo
group (2%) who had a fracture. Femur fractures occurred in three patients treated with
ISEMBYLD in Study 1. No patients discontinued treatment due to fractures. In an ongoing
open-label extension study in which patients received ISEMBYLD 20 mg/kg (twice the
recommended dosage), fractures occurred in 22 patients (9%), including 5 serious events of
femur fractures. Most patients with fractures had histories of low bone density previous
fractures, or contractures. Some fracture events did not have a clear precipitating event (such
as a fall).
Animal studies have also demonstrated adverse effects in bone [see Nonclinical Toxicology
(13.2)].
Use ISEMBYLD with caution in patients with a history of low bone density or multiple
fractures. In patients who experience fractures, weigh the risks and benefits of continuing
ISEMBYLD.

Reference ID: 5867963

10. Recommendations/Risk-Benefit Assessment
The Applicant demonstrated that apitegromab was efficacious for treatment of spinal muscular
atrophy (SMA) in adults and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older who are currently
receiving a survival motor neuron 2 (SMN2) targeted treatment based on the results of a single
adequate and well-controlled trial and confirmatory evidence. There were no safety findings in
the controlled and uncontrolled clinical trials that cannot be mitigated by labeling.
There are no outstanding unresolved issues.
One postmarketing requirement has been issued.
Agreement has been reached with the Applicant on product labeling.
We agree with the review team that this BLA should be approved.

BLA 761463 Resubmission
ISEMBYLD (apitegromab)

1. Executive Summary
1.1.

Product Introduction

SMA is a serious, life-threatening hereditary disease that can result in significant morbidity and
mortality. It is the most common genetic cause of infant mortality.
Apitegromab is a fully human immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4) monoclonal antibody that specifically
binds with high affinity to promyostatin and latent myostatin, which are inactive precursor
forms of myostatin. Apitegromab inhibits the tolloid-mediated cleavage of latent myostatin to it
active form, preventing activation and release of mature myostatin growth factor that inhibits
muscle growth.
The Applicant first submitted BLA 761463 on January 22, 2025. A Complete Response letter was
issued on September 22, 2025, which cited current Good Manufacturing Practice (CGMP) facility
inspection deficiencies. A Type A meeting was held with the Applicant on November 12, 2025
(with meeting minutes dated December 9, 2025). In that meeting, the Agency encouraged the
Applicant to consider other options to allow subjects to continue to receive apitegromab and
consider an alternate drug product manufacturing facility. No clinical deficiencies were identified
during review of BLA 761463. The Applicant also confirmed that they do not plan to provide

1.2.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness

The following conclusions on substantial evidence of effectiveness are based primarily on
information submitted in the original BLA 761463. No new efficacy data were included in this
resubmission.
SAPPHIRE (SRK-015-003) was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter
study in 188 subjects with SMA ages 2 and older who were nonambulatory and on stable
background therapy with either of the currently approved SMN targeting therapies (nusinersen
or risdiplam). Subjects received intravenous (IV) apitegromab doses of 10 mg/kg or 20 mg/kg
every 4 weeks or placebo. The primary endpoint was the mean change from baseline to 12
months in the Hammersmith Functional Motor Scale Expanded (HFMSE). The HFMSE evaluates
CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5867943

9

Clinical Review
Monica Petluru
BLA 761463 Resubmission
ISEMBYLD (apitegromab)
motor function in patients with SMA and is comprised of 33 scored activities that assess motor
ability and clinical progression, such as the ability to sit unassisted, stand, or walk. The
maximum total score is 66, and a higher total score reflects better motor function. The Division
considers change in HFMSE an acceptable primary endpoint to assess clinically meaningful
benefit in a patient with SMA, and it has been used in other clinical trials that supported
approvals of drugs for treatment of SMA. The secondary endpoints were change from baseline
to 12 months in the Revised Upper Limb Module (RULM) and in the World Health Organization
(WHO) Motor Development Milestones, and the proportion of subjects with a ≥3-point change
from baseline in HFMSE at 12 months.
There were two primary efficacy analyses that occurred in the Main Efficacy Population (MEP),
which included subjects who were 2 to 12 years of age and had at least one postbaseline
assessment on the HFMSE. Subjects ages 13 to 21 were included in the Exploratory Population
(EXP). The primary endpoint was the change from baseline in the HFMSE at 12 months analyzed
in the pooled 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose groups compared to placebo and in
the 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose group compared to placebo; analyses were controlled for
multiple comparisons using the Hochberg method.
In the analysis of the pooled 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose groups, there was a
statistically significant and robust difference of 1.8 points in the HFMSE change from baseline to
Month 12 favoring apitegromab (0.6-point least squares [LS] mean in the pooled apitegromab
group versus -1.2-point LS mean in the placebo group; p=0.0192).
In the analysis of the 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose group, there was a numerical difference of
1.4 points in the HFMSE change from baseline to Month 12 favoring apitegromab that was not
statistically significant (p=0.1149). Therefore, all other endpoints were considered exploratory.
However, a prespecified secondary endpoint, the HFMSE change from baseline at 12 months in
the 10 mg/kg apitegromab dose group, was nominally statistically significant, with a LS mean
difference in of 2.2 points favoring apitegromab compared to placebo (nominal p =0.0121). The
results of the primary analyses were generalizable across demographic subgroups of age, sex,
and regions of the world. No single site contributed an unusually large proportion of subjects,
and no site was the main contributor for the observed treatment effect in SAPPHIRE.
The key secondary endpoints of RULM and WHO Motor Milestones showed numerical trends in
favor of apitegromab that did not achieve nominal statistical significance for the pooled group
or the individual 10 mg/kg or 20 mg/kg dose groups. The proportion of clinical responders
(defined as improvement in the HFMSE ≥3 points by Month 12) was numerically higher in the
apitegromab groups compared to those in the placebo group and were nominally significant in
the combined dose group and in the 10 mg/kg dose group, but not in the 20 mg/kg dose group.
The Applicant’s proposed marketed dose is 10 mg/kg every 4 weeks; however, in SAPPHIRE
CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5867943

10

Clinical Review
Monica Petluru
BLA 761463 Resubmission
ISEMBYLD (apitegromab)
subjects in the MEP were randomized to receive 10 mg/kg or 20 mg/kg, and all EXP subjects
received 20 mg/kg. The Office of Clinical Pharmacology evaluated the effect of dose on the
primary endpoint in the MEP and EXP in SAPPHIRE. Their analysis demonstrated separation
between placebo and the individual 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg, which increased the
persuasiveness of the findings. As noted above, there was a greater observed treatment
response on the primary endpoint for 10 mg/kg compared to 20 mg/kg (LS mean differences of
2.2 and 1.4, respectively). This difference was considered to be due to an imbalance between
groups in the number of very young subjects 2 to 3 years of age (4 in the 10 mg/kg group versus
2 in the 20 mg/kg group), whose responses were more variable and had a larger effect size than
subjects 4 to 12 years of age. Subjects in the youngest age group had been on their background
SMN2 therapy for the shortest period of time, and the effect of SMN2 therapy is expected to
plateau after 3 to 4 years. Thus, the impact of SMN2 therapy on the HFMSE outcome may have
been greater in this subpopulation. When all subjects 2 to 3 years of age are excluded from the
primary analysis, the treatment difference for the 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg groups are more
similar. Specifically, there was a numerical difference of 1.5 points favoring apitegromab 20
mg/kg in the HFMSE (p=0.0667) and a numerical difference of 1.6 points favoring
apitegromab 10 mg/kg (nominal p=0.0474).
Confirmatory evidence (CE) is provided from relevant pharmacodynamic and mechanistic data
of latent myostatin concentrations. Apitegromab is hypothesized to selectively bind to
promyostatin and latent myostatin and inhibit the activation of myostatin, blocking myostatin
signaling and preventing the upregulation of muscle-atrophy-related genes and loss of skeletal
muscle mass. The Applicant measured the latent myostatin concentrations in the serum at
different timepoints in both SAPPHIRE and a phase 2 study, TOPAZ. FDA explored the
longitudinal changes (relative to baseline) in the serum latent myostatin concentrations to
determine if latent myostatin could serve as a target engagement biomarker. The longitudinal
changes (relative to baseline) in serum latent myostatin concentrations were comparable
between 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg in subjects 2 to 12 years old, and there is no apparent doseresponse in serum latent myostatin levels at the end of Year 1, demonstrating that there is no
significant difference between the 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg dose. In subjects 13 to 21 years, 20
mg/kg apitegromab showed consistent trends in changes in the latent myostatin levels in serum
as was noted in both of the dose arms in 2- to 12-year-olds. These
pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data contributed to the confirmatory evidence of a treatment
benefit and that the drug is having its intended effect on the muscle.
The FDA draft guidance for industry Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for
Human Drug and Biological Products (June 2026)1 outlines the general requirements for the
determination of substantial evidence of effectiveness, the situations in which a single
adequate and well-controlled study could adequately support an effectiveness claim, and cites
1 https://www.fda.gov/media/133660/download

CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5867943

Clinical Review
Monica Petluru
BLA 761463 Resubmission
ISEMBYLD (apitegromab)
the ability of the Agency to also consider data from one adequate and well-controlled clinical
investigation and confirmatory evidence to constitute substantial evidence of effectiveness.
Although there are available therapies, there remains an unmet need for safe and effective
therapies for SMA. SAPPHIRE is a single, adequate, and well-controlled study that won on a
clinically relevant primary endpoint, with favorable trends on the secondary endpoints. In the
context of this rare, progressive, and serious, life-threatening disease with unmet need, it is
appropriate to rely on a single study plus CE. Therefore, the statistically significant results on a
clinically meaningful endpoint (HFMSE), and multiple supplementary analyses, along with
confirmatory evidence from relevant pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data provide independent
substantiation of the results from the SAPPHIRE study. Together, the data provide substantial
evidence of effectiveness of apitegromab for the adjunctive treatment of pediatric and adult
patients with SMA who are on approved SMN2-targeted therapies (nusinersen or risdiplam).
Overall, there were no major safety concerns that would preclude approval. Findings in the
single adequate and well-controlled study indicate potential concerns for increased risk of
fractures, which should be described in the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling, and
risk of elevated creatine kinase (CK), both of which can be mitigated through patient labeling.
Enhanced pharmacovigilance will also be requested to further characterize and follow the risk
for bone fractures.
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence. The Applicant submitted results
from a single, positive, adequate, well-controlled study (SAPPHIRE). Additionally, confirmatory
evidence comes from pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data on latent myostatin, a target
engagement biomarker. Together, these data provide substantial evidence of effectiveness of
apitegromab for the adjunctive treatment of pediatric and adult patients with SMA ages 2 and
up who are also being treated with approved SMN2 targeted therapies.

1.3.

Benefit-Risk Assessment

CDER Clinical Review Template

ISEMBYLD (apitegromab)
Benefit-Risk Integrated Assessment
The benefit-risk assessment is extracted from the original integrated clinical review of BLA
761463, dated September 22, 2025.
SMA is a serious, life-threatening hereditary disease that can result in significant morbidity
and mortality. It is the most common genetic cause of infant mortality. Currently, there are 3
FDA-approved therapies for SMA: two SMN2-targeted therapies (nusinersen and risdiplam),
and one gene therapy (onasemnogene abeparvovec) that replaces SMN1. There remains a
significant unmet clinical need for effective treatments for patients with SMA because not all
patients are able to receive, tolerate, or adequately benefit from the three currently
approved drugs. None of the existing treatments for SMA addresses muscle atrophy.
Apitegromab is an intravenously administered fully human immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4)
monoclonal antibody that specifically binds with high affinity to promyostatin and latent
myostatin, which are inactive precursor forms of myostatin. Apitegromab inhibits the tolloidmediated cleavage of latent myostatin to it active form, preventing activation and release of
mature myostatin growth factor that inhibits muscle growth.
Based on positive results of a single adequate and well-controlled study (SAPPHIRE) and
confirmatory evidence from the pharmacodynamic and mechanistic data, the effectiveness
of apitegromab has been established for the adjunctive treatment of adult and pediatric
patients ages 2 years and older with SMA who are on SMN2-targeted therapies (nusinersen
and risdiplam).
There is residual uncertainty as to benefit in patients who have received onasemnogene
abeparvovec, an SMN1-targeted gene therapy, as these patients were excluded from
SAPPHIRE.
For this rare disease with unmet medical need, a single adequate and well-controlled study
plus confirmatory evidence are sufficient to provide SEE for apitegromab in the treatment of
SMA in patients 2 years of age and older who are receiving SMN2-targeted therapies.
The safety database for apitegromab was adequate for the intended population and
proposed dosing regimen. The safety database was characterized in SAPPHIRE, a 52-week
randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multinational phase 3 study, as well as with
an open-label study SRK-015-004 (ONYX). In subjects who received apitegromab 10 mg/kg in
the MEP of SAPPHIRE, the most frequently reported (≥5% frequency and ≥5% more
frequently than in placebo) treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) vs placebo were
upper respiratory tract infections, vomiting, cough, other viral infections, headache,
gastroenteritis, pharyngitis, rash, diarrhea, injection site reactions, abdominal pain,
CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5867943

13

Clinical Review
Monica Petluru
BLA 761463 Resubmission
ISEMBYLD (apitegromab)
arthralgia, contusion, fall, fractures, fatigue, pain in extremity, hematoma, and myalgia. The
SAEs that occurred in more than one subject include pneumonia, dehydration, and acute
respiratory failure. There were no adverse events that led to treatment discontinuation in
SAPPHIRE. There were no deaths in either SAPPHIRE or ONYX.
Safety signals identified during SAPPHIRE include possible increased risk for bone fractures
and elevated creatine kinase levels. These risks can be adequately addressed through
labeling and through routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance. Warnings and Precautions
statements will include the risk of fractures. In addition, enhanced pharmacovigilance will be
recommended for bone fractures. A pregnancy outcomes study will be a postmarketing
requirement.
From the Safety Update data presented in this Class II resubmission of BLA 761463, no new
safety signals have been identified.
The conclusion of this review is that substantial evidence of effectiveness has been
established. The safety profile of apitegromab from the clinical trials is generally acceptable
given the seriousness of SMA and can be mitigated with warnings and other information in
the labeling.

Patient Experience Data

2. Therapeutic Context
2.1.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options

There are three FDA-approved therapies for the treatment of SMA. Nusinersen (Spinraza), an
intrathecally administered SMN2 targeted antisense oligonucleotide, was approved for the
treatment of SMA in pediatric and adult patients in 2016. Onasemnogene abeparvovec-xioi
(Zolgensma), a single dose gene-replacement therapy that uses a nonreplicating adenoassociated virus (AAV) capsid to deliver a functional copy of the SMN1 gene by intravenous
infusion, was approved by the FDA in 2019 for patients with SMA <2 years of age. Risdiplam, an
orally administered SMN2 splicing modifier, was approved by the FDA in 2020 for pediatric and
adult patients with SMA.
See original integrated review for BLA 761463 dated September 22, 2025 for further details.

8. Review of Safety
8.1. Safety Review Approach
See the original integrated review for BLA 761463 dated September 22, 2025, for a detailed
discussion of the safety data provided to support the 10mg/kg IV q4week dose of apitegromab
(last data cutoff for original BLA submission at time of 90 day safety update was January 10,
2025).
To support this Class II BLA resubmission, the Applicant provided additional safety data from
the ongoing OLE study ONYX (data cutoff August 29, 2025) as well as additional safety
information for AEs and SAEs from ONYX, the ongoing EAP, and limited information from the
ongoing study OPAL (children <2 years of age with SMA).

8.2. Review of the Safety Database
8.2.1. Overall Exposure
As of the data cutoff of August 29, 2025, there have been 246 subjects with SMA exposed to
apitegromab across TOPAZ, SAPPHIRE, and ONYX (both TOPAZ and SAPPHIRE fed into ONYX);
238 subjects have been exposed for at least 1 year, and 49 have been exposed for 5 years. In
ONYX, 233 subjects have received at least one dose of apitegromab, and 224 subjects have

8.4.3. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
During the reporting period of January 10, 2025 to February 15, 2026 in ONYX, no subjects in
ONYX discontinued apitegromab due to adverse events. During the reporting period of August
30, 2025 to February 15, 2026 in ONYX, one subject discontinued treatment, and 2 subjects
discontinued from the study (withdrawal by subject). In the EAP, one subject discontinued due
to a nonserious grade 2 AE of paresthesia, which resolved without treatment. In OPAL, other
than the previously discussed single fatal case, there have been no SAEs or AEs leading to
treatment discontinuation as of February 15, 2026.
8.4.4. Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
In the reporting period of January 10, 2025 to August 29, 2025 in ONYX, the most common
TEAEs per the Applicant analysis (≥10%) were upper respiratory tract infection/nasopharyngitis,
cough, pyrexia, and influenza. The majority of AEs were mild to moderate in severity. In the
reporting period of August 30, 2025 to February 15, 2026, the most common AEs per the
Applicant analysis occurring in at least 20 subjects or more were upper respiratory
infection/nasopharyngitis and pyrexia. TEAE listings by system organ class and preferred term
were also independently evaluated by this reviewer.
In the reporting period with a data cutoff of February 15, 2026, in the EAP, the most common
AEs that occurred in 5 or more subjects (≥3%), per the Applicant analysis, included vomiting,
cough, headache, pyrexia, acute respiratory failure, and pneumonia. The majority of AEs were
mild to moderate in severity. TEAE listings by system organ class and preferred term were also

Of note, in SAPPHIRE there had been one mild, non-serious case of urticaria in the apitegromab
10mg/kg group, but in the reporting period for this Class II resubmission, there has been two
additional non-serious cases of urticaria, 1 in ONYX and 1 in the EAP. To better characterize the
potential for hypersensitivity reactions with apitegromab, the label will have a grouping for
“hypersensitivity” in the adverse reaction table to include rashes and urticaria (see section
10.1).
8.4.5. Laboratory Findings
See the original integrated review for BLA 761463 for a discussion of chemistry and hematology
laboratory results. The following discussion is based on data from ONYX in this resubmission;
laboratory data from OPAL and EAP were not provided.
Table 8 below summarizes the updated renal function laboratory values in ONYX in the
reporting period for the Class II resubmission. There continue to be no clinically significant
abnormalities in subjects receiving apitegromab in ONYX. It should be noted that estimated
glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) was estimated using cystatin C instead of creatinine (as was
used in the original integrated review for BLA 761463) because creatinine levels are often
reduced in patients with neuromuscular disorders such as SMA, which may lead to
overestimation of the eGFR.

ruled out, it does not appear to be likely, as these subjects had reductions and/or fluctuations

(such as concurrent gastrointestinal illness or hepatic hemangioma). Additionally, one subject
had an AE of “hepatic function abnormal” but the liver function testing for this subject was

8.5.

Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues

In the original integrated review for BLA 761463, the key safety review issues identified were
increased risk of fractures and elevations in creatine kinase (CK). Below are updates on these
key safety review issues during this reporting period for this Class II resubmission.
8.5.1. Fractures
Overall, from January 10, 2025 to February 15, 2026 in ONYX, 14 subjects experienced fractures
(including 2 subjects who had had previous fractures in ONYX and 1 subject who had had a
previous fracture in SAPPHIRE from prior to January 10, 2025). Throughout all of ONYX, in total,
there have been 22 subjects with fractures (9%), including 5 SAEs of femur fractures.
From January 10, 2025 to August 29, 2025, in ONYX, 11 subjects experienced at least 1 fracture
event (most frequent types that occurred in at least 2 subjects included femur, humerus, and
tibia fractures). Of these subjects, 3 had previously had other fractures prior to January 10,
2025, as discussed above. From August 30, 2025 to February 15, 2026 in ONYX, 5 subjects
experienced at least 1 fracture event (most common being femur fractures). Of these subjects,
2 had previously had other fractures between January 10, 2025 to August 29, 2025. (One
(b) (6)
subject,
throughout their entire apitegromab exposure, had multiple femur fractures
as well as a humerus fracture). None of these subjects discontinued ONYX due to their fracture
events.
No subjects in the EAP experienced fractures (of note, the EAP enrolled non-ambulatory
subjects, similar to SAPPHIRE).
The prescribing information will include a Warnings and Precautions for fractures. Additionally,
based on the observed severity and potential seriousness of the fracture events, enhanced
pharmacovigilance for serious and non-serious fracture events will be recommended in patients
with SMA treated with apitegromab.
8.5.2. Creatine Kinase (CK)
Table 12 below summarizes the number of subjects in ONYX with abnormal CK results, and
Table 13 summarizes the shifts from baseline in CK values during the reporting period for this
resubmission; overall these results are consistent with what was reviewed previously in the
CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5867943

33

Clinical Review
Monica Petluru
BLA 761463 Resubmission
ISEMBYLD (apitegromab)
original integrated review of BLA 761463. There continued to be no events of rhabdomyolysis.
The mechanism of action and clinical significance of CK increases in apitegromab-treated
subjects compared to placebo is unknown. Apitegromab, as an inhibitor of precursor forms of
myostatin, may promote muscle growth, but increases in CK generally suggest increased muscle
damage. While these events of CK increases were generally mild, nonserious, and did not lead
to treatment discontinuations, given the discrepancy in the frequency of CK increases in
apitegromab-treated subjects, CK increases will be included in Section 6 of the label of
apitegromab under Laboratory Findings.

8.8.

Additional Safety Explorations

8.8.1. Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
The risk of malignancy with apitegromab is unknown. See also original integrated review for

There were no pregnancies in the reporting period for this Class II BLA resubmission. See also
original integrated review for BLA 761463.

See original integrated review for BLA 761463.

Not applicable.

8.9.

Safety in the Postmarket Setting

8.9.1. Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
Not applicable. Apitegromab is a new molecular entity. It is not approved in the United States
or any foreign market.
8.9.2. Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
The safety and effectiveness of apitegromab in women who are pregnant or breastfeeding
have not been adequately characterized in clinical trials. The risk of malignancy is unknown.

8.10.

Integrated Assessment of Safety

Overall, there are no new clinical safety concerns or signals that have been identified in the
additional safety information provided in this Class II BLA resubmission, and the overall safety
profile has not changed from the original integrated review of BLA 761463. Additionally, given
that fracture risk continues to be significant, enhanced pharmacovigilance for serious and
nonserious fracture events will be recommended in patients with SMA treated with
apitegromab, as previously discussed in the original integrated review for BLA 761463.

9. Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
An advisory committee meeting was not necessary as this is a Class II BLA resubmission. An
advisory committee meeting was also not held during the original BLA review; see original
integrated review of BLA 761463 dated September 22, 2025.

The Division disagreed and deleted
and replaced it with
“lower than” and provided the following rationale: “We cannot agree at this time with your
(b) (4)
proposal to
as no
justification has been provided that this extrapolation accurately reflects risk during the
relevant developmental window. For reproductive and developmental toxicology studies, the
exposure achieved at each interval within the study window characterizes risk at every critical
moment of development, making a time-matched comparison more scientifically appropriate.
We recommend comparing the lowest observed animal AUC₀₋₁₆₈ₕ across a relevant nonclinical
study to the highest human AUC₀₋₁₆₈ₕ over any comparable 7-day clinical dosing interval to
ensure the labeled safety margin reflects a conservative characterization of relative exposure
during the critical window of development, unless an adequate justification can be provided
otherwise.
(b) (4)

(e.g., 50,100 h*ug/mL ÷ 119,520 h*ug/mL = 0.4)”
Section 13.2 Animal Toxicology
The Applicant proposed to add
” in the
following statement: “A no-effect dose for bone effects was not identified. The lowest effect
doses for adverse effects on bone in 12- and 26-week studies in rats (10 and 30 mg/kg,
(b) (4)
respectively) were associated with apitegromab-mstn exposures (AUC)
that in humans at the MRHD.”
(b) (4)

The Division disagreed and deleted
and replaced it
with “lower than” and provided the following rationale: “See comments in sections 8.1 and 8.4
(b) (4)
re: need for additional pharmacokinetic justification to support the proposal to
(b) (4)

Section 14 Clinical Studies
Previously the demographics for the main efficacy population had reflected only the 10mg/kg
apitegromab arm (n=53). The demographics for the main efficacy population have now been
modified to reflect both the 10mg/kg apitegromab arm with the placebo arm (n=50), so total
n=103.

12.

Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments

The Agency has determined that the following postmarketing requirement (PMR) is necessary:
4891-1 -Establish a worldwide single-arm pregnancy safety study to collect and analyze
information for a minimum of 10 years on pregnancy complications and birth outcomes for
exposed pregnancies, including women exposed to apetigromab-mstn during pregnancy
and a relevant exposure period prior to the start of pregnancy. Provide a complete protocol
that includes details regarding how you plan to encourage patients and providers to report
pregnancy exposures, measures to ensure complete data capture regarding pregnancy
outcomes and any adverse effects in offspring, and plans for comprehensive data analysis.
The timetable submitted on August 25, 2026, states that the Applicant will conduct this
study according to the following schedule:
Draft Protocol Submission:
Final Protocol Submission:
Annual Interim Report Submission:
Study Completion:
Final Report Submission:

I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
The Applicant (Scholar Rock, Inc.) submitted BLA 761463 for apitegromab (Isembyld).
Apitegromab is a fully human immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4) monoclonal antibody that specifically
binds to inactive precursor forms of myostatin and is indicated for the treatment of adult and
pediatric patients with spinal muscular atrophy (SMA).
BLA 761463 was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. The review team determined the
effectiveness of apitegromab 10 mg/kg was established for the treatment of SMA and the safety
profile of the drug based on the clinical trial data appeared acceptable. However, the Office of
Product Quality (OPQ) team identified approvability issues and recommends a Complete
Response action. The signatory authority for this application concurs with OPQ’s
recommendation for a Complete Response.
The submission contains efficacy and safety results from a single adequate and well-controlled
study, SRK-015-003 (SAPPHIRE), and confirmatory evidence (CE) of biomarker and clinical
data.
SAPPHIRE was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter study in 188
subjects with SMA ages 2 and older who were nonambulatory and on stable background therapy
with either of the currently approved survival motor neuron 2 (SMN2) targeting therapies
(nusinersen or risdiplam). Subjects received intravenous (IV) apitegromab doses of 10 mg/kg or
20 mg/kg every 4 weeks or placebo. The primary endpoint was the mean change from baseline to
12 months in the Hammersmith Functional Motor Scale Expanded (HFMSE). The HFMSE
evaluates motor function in patients with SMA and is comprised of 33 scored activities that
assess motor ability and clinical progression, such as the ability to sit unassisted, stand, or walk.
The maximum total score is 66, and a higher total score reflects better motor function. The
Division considers change in HFMSE an acceptable primary endpoint to assess clinically
meaningful benefit in a patient with SMA, and it has been used in other clinical trials that
supported approvals of drugs for treatment of SMA. The secondary endpoints were change from
baseline to 12 months in the Revised Upper Limb Module and in the World Health Organization
(WHO) Motor Development Milestones, and the proportion of subjects with a ≥3-point change
from baseline in HFMSE at 12 months.
There were two primary efficacy analyses that occurred in the Main Efficacy Population (MEP),
which included subjects who were 2 to 12 years of age and had at least one postbaseline
assessment on the HFMSE. Subjects ages 13 to 21 were included in the Exploratory Population
(EXP). The primary endpoint was the change from baseline in the HFMSE at 12 months
analyzed in the pooled 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose groups compared to placebo
and in the 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose group compared to placebo; analyses were controlled for
multiple comparisons using the Hochberg method.
4
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
In the analysis of the pooled 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose groups, there was a
statistically significant and robust difference of 1.8 points in the HFMSE change from baseline to
Month 12 favoring apitegromab (0.6-point least squares [LS] mean in the pooled apitegromab
group versus -1.2-point LS mean in the placebo group; p=0.0192).
In the analysis of the 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose group, there was a numerical difference of 1.4
points in the HFMSE change from baseline to Month 12 favoring apitegromab that was not
statistically significant (p=0.1149). Therefore, all other endpoints were considered exploratory.
However, a prespecified secondary endpoint, the HFMSE change from baseline at 12 months in
the 10 mg/kg apitegromab dose group, was nominally statistically significant, with a LS mean
difference in of 2.2 points favoring apitegromab compared to placebo (nominal p =0.0121). The
results of the primary analyses were generalizable across demographic subgroups of age, sex,
and regions of the world. No single site contributed an unusually large proportion of subjects,
and no site was the main contributor for the observed treatment effect in SAPPHIRE.
The key secondary endpoints of Revised Upper Limb Module (RULM) and World Health
Organization (WHO) Motor Milestones showed numerical trends in favor of apitegromab that
did not achieve nominal statistical significance for either the pooled or individual 10 mg/kg and
20 mg/kg dose groups. The proportion of clinical responders (defined as improvement in the
HFMSE ≥3 points by Month 12) was numerically higher in the apitegromab groups compared to
those in the placebo group and met nominal statistical significance in the combined dose group
and in the 10 mg/kg dose group, but not in the 20 mg/kg dose group.
The Applicant’s proposed marketed dose is 10 mg/kg every 4 weeks; however, in SAPPHIRE
subjects in the MEP were randomized to receive 10 mg/kg or 20 mg/kg, and all EXP subjects
received 20 mg/kg. The Office of Clinical Pharmacology evaluated the effect of dose on the
primary endpoint in the MEP and EXP in SAPPHIRE. Their analysis demonstrated separation
between placebo and the individual 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg, which increased the persuasiveness
of the findings. As noted above, there was a greater observed treatment response on the primary
endpoint for 10 mg/kg compared to 20 mg/kg (LS mean differences of 2.2 and 1.4, respectively).
This difference was considered to be due to an imbalance between groups in the number of very
young subjects 2 to 3 years of age (4 in the 10 mg/kg group versus 2 in the 20 mg/kg group),
whose responses were more variable and had a larger effect size than subjects 4 to 12 years of
age. Subjects in the youngest age group had been on their background SMN2 therapy for the
shortest period of time, and the effect of SMN2 therapy is expected to plateau after 3 to 4 years.
Thus, the impact of SMN2 therapy on the HFMSE outcome may have been greater in this
subpopulation. When all subjects 2 to 3 years of age are excluded from the primary analysis, the
treatment difference for the 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg groups are more similar. Specifically, there
was a numerical difference of 1.5 points favoring apitegromab 20 mg/kg in the HFMSE
(p=0.0667) and a numerical difference of 1.6 points favoring apitegromab 10 mg/kg (nominal
p=0.0474).
CE is provided from relevant pharmacodynamic and mechanistic data of latent myostatin
concentrations. Apitegromab is hypothesized to selectively bind to promyostatin and latent
myostatin and inhibit the activation of myostatin, blocking myostatin signaling and preventing
the upregulation of muscle-atrophy-related genes and loss of skeletal muscle mass. The
Applicant measured the latent myostatin concentrations in the serum at different timepoints in
both SAPPHIRE and a phase 2 study, TOPAZ. FDA explored the longitudinal changes (relative
5
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
to baseline) in the serum latent myostatin concentrations to determine if latent myostatin could
serve as a target engagement biomarker. The longitudinal changes (relative to baseline) in serum
latent myostatin concentrations were comparable between 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg in subjects 2
to 12 years old, and there is no apparent dose-response in serum latent myostatin levels at the end
of Year 1, demonstrating that there is no significant difference between the 10 mg/kg and 20
mg/kg dose. In subjects 13 to 21 years, 20 mg/kg apitegromab showed consistent trends in
changes in the latent myostatin levels in serum as was noted in both of the dose arms in 2- to 12year-olds. These pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data contributed to the confirmatory evidence of
a treatment benefit and that the drug is having its intended effect on the muscle.
The FDA draft guidance for industry Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for
Human Drug and Biological Products (December 2019) outlines the general requirements for
the determination of substantial evidence of effectiveness, the situations in which a single
adequate and well-controlled study could adequately support an effectiveness claim, and cites the
ability of the Agency to also consider data from one adequate and well-controlled clinical
investigation and confirmatory evidence to constitute substantial evidence of effectiveness.
Although there are available therapies, there remains an unmet need for safe and effective
therapies for SMA. SAPPHIRE is a single, adequate, and well-controlled study that won on a
clinically relevant primary endpoint, with favorable trends on the secondary endpoints. In the
context of this rare, progressive, and serious, life-threatening disease with unmet need, it is
appropriate to rely on a single study plus CE. Therefore, the statistically significant results on a
clinically meaningful endpoint (HFMSE), and multiple supplementary analyses, along with
confirmatory evidence from relevant pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data provide independent
substantiation of the results from the SAPPHIRE study. Together, the data provide substantial
evidence of effectiveness of apitegromab for the adjunctive treatment of pediatric and adult
patients with SMA who are on approved SMN2-targeted therapies (nusinersen or risdiplam).
Overall, there were no major safety concerns that would preclude approval. Findings in the
single adequate and well-controlled study indicate potential concerns for increased risk of
fractures, which should be described in the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling, and
risk of elevated creatine phosphokinase (CPK), both of which can be mitigated through patient
labeling. Enhanced pharmacovigilance will also be requested to further characterize and follow
the risk for bone fractures.
(b) (4) drug product
During the inspection of the
manufacturing facility for this application, investigators conveyed deficiencies to the
representative of the facility. The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) does not consider this
BLA approvable until these issues are addressed.

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
The Applicant submitted results from a single, positive, adequate, well-controlled study
(SAPPHIRE). Additionally, confirmatory evidence comes from pharmacodynamic/mechanistic
6

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
data on latent myostatin, a target engagement biomarker. Together, these data provide substantial
evidence of effectiveness of apitegromab for the adjunctive treatment of pediatric and adult
patients with SMA ages 2 and up who are also being treated with approved SMN2 targeted
therapies.

Conclusions and Reasons

SMA is an autosomal recessive disease with survival motor neuron (SMN) protein deficiency SMA is a rare, serious, lifethreatening disease that can
that causes motor neuron loss, leading to weakness and muscle atrophy. It is the most
common genetic cause of infant mortality, with an estimated global incidence of 8 per 100,000 cause morbidity and mortality
due to muscle weakness and
live births (Coratti et al. 2023). Death in SMA is usually due to respiratory failure.
There are different types of SMA (types 0, 1, 2, 3, and 4). Classification into SMA types used respiratory failure.
to be based only on the age when symptoms began and the motor milestones that patients
could ultimately achieve. Genetic testing is now used to help classify the different types of
SMA. The more copies of SMN2 that a patient has, the better the prognosis.
Symptoms of SMA vary based on the type of SMA and age of onset, but include loss of motor
milestone development. Affected infants have impaired motor milestone development, and
difficulty or inability to lift the head, sit, stand, or walk. Patients with SMA may have difficulty
with swallowing and respiration. Additionally, patients with SMA often have other
comorbidities, such as contractures and scoliosis.
Medical care focuses on respiratory support, nutritional support, the management of resulting
respiratory infections with antibiotics, and the management of resulting tendon contractures
and scoliosis through bracing, physical therapy, and surgery. Although the lifespan of the
most severely affected patients with SMA can be increased through invasive mechanical
ventilation, the quality of such a life is poor and most patients eventually succumb to
respiratory infection.

 There are three FDA-approved drugs for the treatment of SMA.
– Nusinersen (Spinraza), an intrathecally administered SMN2 targeted antisense
oligonucleotide, was approved for the treatment of SMA in pediatric and adult patients in
2016. Nusinersen must be re-administered intrathecally every 4 months, but is difficult to
use in patients with poor spinal access or significant anxiety about repeated spinal
injection. Repeated lumbar punctures also carry risks of headache, hemorrhage, and
infection.
– Onasemnogene abeparvovec (Zolgensma), a gene-replacement therapy that uses a
nonreplicating adeno-associated virus (AAV) capsid to deliver a functional copy of the
SMN gene by intravenous infusion, was approved by the FDA in 2019 for patients with

antibodies preventing onasemnogene treatment increases from ~2% in infants up to 30%
in adults.
– Risdiplam, an orally administered SMN2 splicing modifier, was approved by the FDA in
2020 for pediatric and adult patients with SMA.
 The benefits of nusinersen and risdiplam may stabilize and plateau over time (Oskoui et al.
2023; Gunther et al. 2024).

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
SMA is a serious, life-threatening hereditary disease that can result in significant morbidity and
mortality. It is the most common genetic cause of infant mortality. Currently, there are 3 FDAapproved therapies for SMA: two SMN2-targeted therapies (nusinersen and risdiplam), and one
gene therapy (onasemnogene abeparvovec) that replaces SMN1. There remains a significant
unmet clinical need for effective treatments for patients with SMA because not all patients are
able to receive, tolerate, or adequately benefit from the three currently approved drugs.
Additionally, the benefits of nusinersen and risdiplam may stabilize and plateau over time
(Oskoui et al. 2023; Gunther et al. 2024). None of the existing treatments for SMA addresses
muscle atrophy.
Apitegromab is an intravenously administered fully human immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4)
monoclonal antibody that specifically binds with high affinity to promyostatin and latent
myostatin, which are inactive precursor forms of myostatin. Apitegromab inhibits the tolloidmediated cleavage of latent myostatin to it active form, preventing activation and release of
mature myostatin growth factor that inhibits muscle growth.
Based on positive results of a single adequate and well-controlled study (SAPPHIRE) and
confirmatory evidence from the pharmacodynamic and mechanistic data, the effectiveness of
apitegromab has been established for the adjunctive treatment of adult and pediatric patients ages
2 years and older with SMA who are on SMN2-targeted therapies (nusinersen and risdiplam).
There is residual uncertainty as to benefit in patients who have received onasemnogene
abeparvovec, an SMN1-targeted gene therapy, as these patients were excluded from SAPPHIRE.
For this rare disease with unmet medical need, a single adequate and well-controlled study plus
confirmatory evidence are sufficient to provide SEE for apitegromab in the treatment of SMA in
patients 2 years of age and older who are receiving SMN2-targeted therapies(See Section 1.2).
The safety database for apitegromab was adequate for the intended population and proposed
dosing regimen. The safety database was characterized in SAPPHIRE, a 52-week randomized,
double-blind, placebo-controlled, multinational phase 3 study, as well as with an open-label
study SRK-015-004 (ONYX). In subjects who received apitegromab 10 mg/kg in the MEP of
SAPPHIRE, the most frequently reported (≥5% frequency and ≥5% more frequently than in
placebo) treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) vs placebo were upper respiratory tract
infections, vomiting, cough, other viral infections, headache, gastroenteritis, pharyngitis, rash,
diarrhea, injection site reactions, abdominal pain, arthralgia, contusion, fall, fractures, fatigue,
pain in extremity, hematoma, and myalgia. The SAEs that occurred in more than one subject
include pneumonia, dehydration, and acute respiratory failure. There were no adverse events that
led to treatment discontinuation in SAPPHIRE. There were no deaths in either SAPPHIRE or
ONYX.
Safety signals identified during SAPPHIRE include possible increased risk for bone fractures
and elevated creatine phosphate kinase levels. These risks can be adequately addressed through
labeling and through routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance. Warnings and Precautions
statements will include the risk of fractures. In addition, enhanced pharmacovigilance will be
recommended for bone fractures. A pregnancy outcomes study will be a postmarketing
requirement.
13
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
However, the Office of Product Quality has identified unresolved quality issues with the drug
product manufacturing facility. Deficiencies were identified during an inspection of the facility
and conveyed to the representative of the facility.
The conclusion of this review is that substantial evidence of effectiveness has been established.
The safety profile of apitegromab from the clinical trials is generally acceptable given the
seriousness of SMA and can be mitigated with warnings and other information in the labeling.
However, the risk of apitegromab cannot be fully determined due to unresolved issues with the
drug product manufacturing facility. Therefore, the safety and effectiveness of apitegromab
cannot be established and a complete response will be issued. The Applicant will need to address
the unresolved manufacturing deficiencies to support approval of this product.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Spinal muscular atrophy (SMA) is an autosomal recessive disease with SMN protein deficiency
that causes motor neuron loss in the brainstem and spinal cord, leading to weakness and muscle
atrophy. There are different types of SMA (types 0, 1, 2, 3, and 4). Classification into SMA types
used to be based on the age when symptoms began and the motor milestones that patients would
achieve. Genetic testing is currently used to help classify the different types of SMA. In general,
the more copies of the survival motor neuron 2 gene (SMN2) that a patient has, the better the
prognosis is. Affected infants with SMA type 1 who are untreated have significantly impaired
motor milestone development. For example, they typically remain unable to lift their head, sit,
stand, or walk. Following the initial weakness and impaired motor development, there is a longterm decline or a plateau in strength and movement ability. Patients with later-onset SMA in
childhood, adolescence, or adulthood may have more gradual onset and less severe symptoms of
muscle weakness and atrophy, though symptoms can be variable. SMA can lead to significant
respiratory morbidity and death, generally secondary to respiratory failure at a young age. SMA
is the most common genetic cause of infant mortality, with an estimated global incidence of 8
per 100,000 live births (Coratti et al. 2023).
There are three FDA-approved therapies for the treatment of SMA. Nusinersen (Spinraza), an
intrathecally administered SMN2 targeted antisense oligonucleotide, was approved for the
treatment of SMA in pediatric and adult patients in 2016. Onasemnogene abeparvovec-xioi
(Zolgensma), a single dose gene-replacement therapy that uses a nonreplicating adeno-associated
virus (AAV) capsid to deliver a functional copy of the SMN1 gene by intravenous infusion, was
approved by the FDA in 2019 for patients with SMA <2 years of age. Risdiplam, an orally
administered SMN2s splicing modifier, was approved by the FDA in 2020 for pediatric and adult
patients with SMA. There remains a significant unmet clinical need for effective treatments for
patients with SMA because not all patients are able to receive, tolerate, or adequately benefit
from the three currently approved drugs. Nusinersen must be re-administered intrathecally every
4 months; it is difficult to use in patients with poor spinal access or significant anxiety about
repeated spinal injection. Repeated lumbar punctures also carry risks of headache, hemorrhage,
and infection. The duration of benefit from Zolgensma has not yet been established, and it cannot
be administered more than a single time because of AAV antibody development. Further, the
proportion of patients with pre-existing AAV antibodies preventing Zolgensma treatment
increases from ~2% in infants up to 30% in adults. Additionally, the benefits of nusinersen and
risdiplam may stabilize and plateau over time (Oskoui et al. 2023; Gunther et al. 2024). None of
the existing treatments for SMA address muscle atrophy.
Other medical care is supportive and focuses on respiratory support, nutritional support, the
management of resulting respiratory infections with antibiotics, and the management of resulting
tendon contractures and scoliosis through bracing, physical therapy, and surgery. Although the
lifespan of the most severely affected patients with SMA can be increased through invasive
mechanical ventilation, the quality of such a life is poor and most patients eventually succumb to
respiratory infection.
15
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
Apitegromab is a fully human immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4) monoclonal antibody that specifically
binds with high affinity to promyostatin and latent myostatin, which are inactive precursor forms
of myostatin. Apitegromab inhibits the tolloid-mediated cleavage of latent myostatin to it active
form, preventing activation and release of mature myostatin growth factor that inhibits muscle
growth.
Myostatin, also known as growth and differentiation factor-8 (GDF-8), is a negative regulator of
skeletal muscle development (Gnazzo et al. 2025). It has been shown that some animals with
genetic deficiencies in myostatin present with dramatic increases in skeletal muscle mass,
supporting the role of myostatin in suppressing muscle growth (Lee 2021). Myostatin binds to
cell-surface receptors causing muscle loss. Myostatin circulates in the blood in a latent form as a
full-length precursor, which is cleaved into an amino-terminal pro-peptide and a carboxyterminal mature region: the active form of the molecule. The Applicant hypothesizes that binding
of the precursor of myostatin will lead to reduction in mature myostatin and thus reduction in
loss of muscle mass, increased strength, and improved motor function.

3.1. Review Issue List
3.1.1. Key Efficacy Review Issues
3.1.1.1. Efficacy of the Proposed Marketing Dose
Evaluate whether the evidence provided by the Applicant supports the substantial evidence of
effectiveness for apitegromab as an adjunctive therapy for the treatment of SMA in pediatric and
adult patients 2 years of age and older who are currently on a SMN targeted treatment, and
whether the proposed marketing dose of apitegromab 10 mg/kg administered Q4W is the optimal
dosing regimen.

3.1.2. Key Safety Review Issues
3.1.2.1. Increased Risk of Fractures
3.1.2.2. Increases in Creatine Phosphokinase

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
Efficacy and safety were assessed based on data from the randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled study SAPPHIRE. The primary efficacy assessment focused on SAPPHIRE, with
support from the active-control phase 2 study TOPAZ.
The Applicant submitted as confirmatory evidence biomarker (total serum latent myostatin) data
from SAPPHIRE, as well as 48 months of efficacy data and 12 months of biomarker data from
TOPAZ.

16
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
The safety assessment was based on SAPPHIRE, with supportive evidence from the open-label
extension study ONYX. The evaluation incorporated both the Applicant’s reports and FDA data
analyst and clinical reviewer analysis of the submitted data. The clinical reviewer acknowledges
the data analyses conducted by FDA Clinical Data Scientists Drs. Aaron Waddell and Ling Cao.
In addition, patient experience data helped inform the review of this product. Information from
the Patient Listening Session held on August 4, 2022, enhanced FDA’s understanding of SMA
and unmet medical need (FDA 2024). Patients described the need for combination/add-on
therapies to address treatment-gaps for SMA-affected individuals who have been treated after the
onset of symptoms, as well as those who were treated early in life prior to the onset of
symptoms. It was noted that combination/add-on therapies can help facilitate motor function
gains to increase independence (FDA 2024). For example, one patient described how gaining the
ability to lift the arms just 2 inches made the difference between eating independently and
needing to be fed.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Apitegromab is a fully human IgG4 monoclonal antibody that selectively binds with high affinity
to promyostatin and latent myostatin, which are inactive precursor forms (proforms) of
myostatin. Myostatin (growth and differentiation factor 8, GDF-8) is a negative regulator of
skeletal muscle mass. The binding of apitegromab to myostatin proforms is intended to prevent
release of the active mature myostatin and subsequent binding to its muscle surface receptor. In
in vitro studies, apitegromab bound to pro- and latent myostatin from multiple species (mouse,
rat, rabbit, cynomolgus monkey, and human) with similar high affinity (8.09, 2, 2.5, 4.86, and
3.42nM, respectively), with no detectable binding to mature myostatin, pro- or mature growth
23
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
differentiation factor 11 (GDF11, protein with highest homology to myostatin), pro- or mature
activin A, the mature forms of bone morphogenic protein (BMP) 9, BMP10, or transforming
growth factor beta 1. In in vitro functional assays, apitegromab inhibited proteolytic activation of
promyostatin and latent myostatin with half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) values of
580nM (mouse), 150nM (rat), 644nM (rabbit), 512nM (monkey), and 248nM (human).
In vivo pharmacology studies conducted with apitegromab in severe combined
immunodeficiency (SCID) mice, demonstrated increases in total body lean mass (up to 17%) and
gastrocnemius muscle weight (up to 30%) at all doses tested (1, 3, 10, and 20 mg/kg/week
intraperitoneally (IP) for 4 weeks). In a mouse model of muscle atrophy, a dose of
20 mg/kg/week IP for 2 weeks fully protected mice from the atrophy-inducing effects of
dexamethasone. Inhibition of myostatin activation with muSRK-015P, the parenteral version of
apitegromab on a mouse IgG1 backbone, in combination with a small molecule SMN splice
modulator (SMN-C1), increased muscle size and function in a mouse model of SMA. In
Sprague-Dawley (SD) rats administered a single 10 mg/kg intravenous (IV) dose of apitegromab,
a 15.6% increase in gastrocnemius weight was observed at 28 days postdose.
Weekly IV administration of apitegromab (3, 10, or 30 mg/kg) to monkeys, resulted in increased
gastrocnemius and biceps brachii muscle weights (up to 25%) and increased thigh muscle
volume as assessed by magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) (up to 30%). Analysis of circulating
total latent myostatin, a marker of target engagement for apitegromab demonstrated a substantial
increase over baseline at all doses tested; however, the magnitude of increase was not dosedependent. While apitegromab binds to and engages both pro- and latent forms of myostatin with
similar affinity, only latent myostatin is detected in the serum.

Drug Information
In healthy subjects, apitegromab pharmacokinetics is dose proportional in the range of 1 mg/kg to 30 mg/kg following
single dose, and 10 mg/kg to 30 mg/kg following multiple doses, and in patients with SMA, pharmacokinetics are
dose-proportional in the range of 2 mg/kg to 20 mg/kg.
In study SRK-015-002, following once every 4-week regimen, the accumulation of exposures was reported to be
approximately 2-fold.
Apitegromab exposures reached steady state by approximately 16-20 weeks following once every 4 week dosing.
The to-be-marketed formulation is the same as the clinical study formulation used in the Study SRK-015-003.

Bodyweight-based regimen was evaluated throughout the clinical development program for apitegromab.
No age-based dosage adjustments are needed
No clinical studies were conducted to evaluate the pharmacokinetics of apitegromab in patients with renal or hepatic
impairment. Apitegromab is degraded by proteolytic enzymes and is not expected to undergo renal elimination or
metabolism by hepatic enzymes.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Precipitant)
Apitegromab is neither subject to CYP450 enzymes nor expected to interfere with cytokine levels. Therefore, no
CYP450-mediated drug-interaction studies were conducted.
Apitegromab is a human IgG4 mAb and is not expected to be affected by transporters; therefore, no transportermediated drug-interaction studies were conducted.

Drug Information
Immunogenicity
Immunogenicity assessments were conducted using ECL assay to determine the exposures of antidrug antibodies
(ADA).
In study SRK-015-002, in up to 48 months of treatment, 3/58 (5.2%) of apitegromab treated subjects tested positive
for treatment-emergent anti-apitegromab antibodies at a single timepoint, but none of these samples were analyzed
for neutralizing antibodies.
In study SRK-015-003, in up to 12 months of treatment, 1/128 (0.8%) of apitegromab-treated subjects tested positive
for treatment-emergent anti-apitegromab antibodies at a single time point. No neutralizing antibodies were detected.
Based on the limited number of subjects who tested positive for ADA and neutralizing antibodies, the available data
are too limited to make definitive conclusions regarding an effect on pharmacokinetics, safety, or efficacy of
apitegromab.
