6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
The Applicant conducted a 2-part, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, Phase 1
clinical study (SRK-015-001), as a first-in-human study to evaluate safety, tolerability,
immunogenicity, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics in 66 healthy adult volunteers. In
Part A, the Applicant evaluated single doses of 1 mg/kg (N=6), 3 mg/kg (N=6), 10 mg/kg (N=6),
20 mg/kg (N=6) and 30 mg/kg (N=6), and placebo (N=10). The Applicant noted that following a
single dose of ≥1 mg/kg, serum total latent myostatin concentrations increased by >100-fold and
concluded successful target engagement. They also reported that at dose levels >3 mg/kg,
increases in the serum total latent myostatin concentrations appear to plateau and higher doses
(>10 mg/kg) were associated with longer duration of serum total latent myostatin concentrations
sustained at plateau. After a single dose of apitegromab at 10, 20, and 30 mg/kg, the serum total
latent myostatin concentrations were maintained at a plateau through Days 28, 84, and 112,
respectively. The Applicant noted that these observations suggest that dosing ≥10 mg/kg may
offer sustained target saturation.
In Part B, the Applicant evaluated multiple doses of 10 mg/kg (N=8), 20 mg/kg (N=6) and
30 mg/kg (N=6), or matching placebo (N=6), administered three times, biweekly on Days 0, 14,
and 28. The Applicant noted that following 3 total doses of apitegromab at 10, 20, and 30 mg/kg
given Q2W, serum total latent myostatin concentrations increased by >100-fold, and concluded
successful target engagement. The Applicant also reported that the maximum serum total latent
myostatin concentrations plateaued in all dose groups by Day 28; however, greater clearance
(CL) of total latent myostatin was observed after the last dose of 10 mg/kg, while the 20 mg/kg
and 30 mg/kg dose groups had a more sustained saturation of the total latent myostatin up to Day
140 of the study. The Applicant noted this observation suggested that apitegromab dosing at
≥20 mg/kg may offer sustained target saturation.
The Applicant also conducted a phase 2 proof-of-concept TOPAZ study to evaluate safety and
efficacy in subjects with Type 2 and Type 3 SMA subjects who were 2 to 21 years of age,
receiving apitegromab either as monotherapy or adjunctive therapy. Refer to Section 15 for
additional discussion on TOPAZ study. In Cohort 3 of TOPAZ, 20 subjects with Type 2 SMA,
≥2 years of age and already receiving approved SMN2-targeted therapy (initiated before the
subject turned 5 years of age), were randomized 1:1 to 2 mg/kg or 20 mg/kg Q4W apitegromab
as adjunctive therapy. The Applicant noted dose-dependent target engagement in Cohort 3
during the 52-week treatment period, with greater increases in serum total latent myostatin
observed in subjects administered apitegromab at 20 mg/kg compared to 2 mg/kg. The Applicant
also indicated that by Week 100 (Day 700), when all Cohort 3 subjects assigned to apitegromab
2 mg/kg had transitioned to the 20 mg/kg dose level after 52-weeks, similar serum total latent
myostatin concentrations between the 2 groups were observed.
Based on the pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) data from the first-in-human study
(SRK-015-001) and topline 12-month efficacy results in TOPAZ, the Applicant included
10 mg/kg dose in SAPPHIRE in addition to the planned 20 mg/kg dose. The Applicant expected
28
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
the drug exposures in the 10 mg/kg dose group to increase to a greater extent than the 2 mg/kg
dose, but to a lesser extent than the 20 mg/kg dose, as well as further increase target engagement
(serum latent myostatin) compared with the 2 mg/kg dose but potentially to a lesser degree than
the 20 mg/kg dose. In summary, based on these considerations, the Applicant selected 10 mg/kg
and 20 mg/kg Q4W for evaluation in subjects with SMA aged 2 to 12 years in the main efficacy
population and 20 mg/kg in subjects with SMA aged 13 to 21 years in the exploratory population
in the SAPPHIRE study.

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
6.2.1. Study SRK-015-003, SAPPHIRE
6.2.1.1. Design, SAPPHIRE
SAPPHIRE was a phase 3, 52-week, pivotal, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study
to evaluate the efficacy and safety of apitegromab in subjects with SMA 2 to 21 years of age and
receiving background nusinersen or risdiplam therapy. The study enrolled 188 subjects (target
sample size was 204) with nonambulatory Type 2 or Type 3 SMA, including 156 subjects ages 2
to 12 years (main efficacy population [MEP]) and 32 subjects (target sample size was 48) ages
13 to 21 years (exploratory subpopulation [EXP]) (Figure 1).
Subjects 2 to 12 years of age were randomized (1:1:1) to receive placebo or apitegromab
10 mg/kg or apitegromab 20 mg/kg Q4W. Randomization was stratified by type of background
therapy (i.e., nusinersen or risdiplam) and age at initiation of SMN2 upregulator therapy (≥5
years and <5 years).
Subjects 13 to 21 years old were randomized (1:2) to receive placebo or apitegromab 20 mg/kg
Q4W, respectively. Randomization was stratified by type of background therapy (i.e., nusinersen
or risdiplam).
The total trial participation time for each subject was approximately 76 weeks (approximately 18
months) and consisted of approximately 4 weeks for Screening, 52 weeks (i.e., 12 months) of
trial visits, and 20 weeks of safety follow-up.

Endpoints for SAPPHIRE
Primary Efficacy Endpoint
The primary efficacy endpoint of SAPPHIRE was the change from baseline to Month 12 in the
HFMSE total score in the MEP analyzed in the pooled 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose
groups compared to placebo and in the 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose group compared to placebo.
This scale was also used to assess efficacy in the EXP. The HFMSE, a tool used to assess motor
function in children that has been validated for use in SMA (Glanzman et al. 2011), was used to
evaluate motor function in all subjects. The maximum score for this test is 66; higher scores
indicate better motor function.
The HFMSE is considered to assess clinically meaningful changes in patient function impacted
by SMA. The Division considers the change from baseline on the HFMSE total score as an
acceptable primary endpoint to assess clinically meaningful benefit in patients with SMA, and
the use of the HFMSE as the primary efficacy endpoint for SAPPHIRE was previously agreed
upon in the End of Phase 2 meeting on August 5, 2021.3 There is also regulatory precedent for
the use of the HFMSE as a clinical endpoint in SMA as it was used in the clinical studies that led
to the approval of nusinersen for later-onset SMA (as a primary endpoint) and to the approval of
risdiplam for SMA (as a secondary endpoint).

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

Isembyld (apitegromab)
Secondary Endpoints
Secondary endpoints assessed the RULM and the WHO Motor Milestones in the 10 mg/kg and
20 mg/kg dose groups separately and in the MEP.
Change from baseline in the total score of the RULM at Month 12 was assessed. The RULM is a
tool used to assess motor performance of the upper limb in patients with SMA. It consists of 20
items that test proximal and distal motor functions of the arm. The total score ranges from 0, if
all the items cannot be performed, to 37, if all the activities are achieved fully without any
compensatory maneuvers (Mazzone et al. 2017; Pera et al. 2019).
The WHO motor milestones are a set of 6 milestones in motor development that would be
expected to be attained in healthy children by 24 months of age. The individual milestones are:
sitting without support, standing with assistance, hands and knees crawling, walking with
assistance, standing alone, and walking alone (WHO Multicentre Growth Reference Study
Group 2006).
Figure 2. Windows of Achievement for Six Gross Motor Milestones

Source: WHO Multicentre Growth Reference Study Group 2006
Abbreviation: WHO, World Health Organization (WHO Multicentre Growth Reference Study Group 2006)

The Division has previously accepted the change from baseline in the RULM and WHO Motor
Milestones as secondary endpoints in studies leading to approvals of other drugs to treat SMA.
The change from baseline to Month 12 in the Hammersmith Functional Motor Scale Expanded
(HFMSE) total score in the MEP in the 10 mg/kg apitegromab dose group compared to placebo
was also assessed as a secondary endpoint.

6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, SAPPHIRE
The criteria listed below from the submitted protocol appear adequate to enroll subjects with
SMA representative of the U.S. population.
Inclusion Criteria
To participate in this study, subjects were required to meet the following eligibility criteria:
1. Ages 2 to 21 years old at Screening, male and female.
2. Estimated life expectancy of >2 years from Screening.
3. Documented diagnosis of 5q SMA.
4. Diagnosed with later-onset SMA (i.e., Type 2 and Type 3 SMA) before receiving an
approved SMN upregulator therapy (either nusinersen or risdiplam). Subjects who never had
the ability to walk independently will be classified as Type 2. Subjects who previously had
the ability to walk unaided will be classified as Type 3.
5. Must be nonambulatory at Screening. Nonambulatory subjects must be able to sit
independently (sits up straight with head erect for at least 10 seconds; does not use arms or
hands to balance body or support position) per WHO motor milestones definition at
Screening. Nonambulatory subjects are further defined as not having the ability to
independently ambulate without aids or orthotics over 10 steps at time of walk test during
Screening.
6. Receiving one background therapy for SMA (either nusinersen or risdiplam) for the time
period specified below and anticipated to maintain that same treatment throughout the study:
a. If receiving nusinersen, must have completed at least 10 months of dosing (i.e.,
completed the loading regimen and at least 2 maintenance doses) before Screening
b. If receiving risdiplam, must have completed at least 6 months of dosing before Screening
7. Motor Function Score (HFMSE) ≥10 and ≤45 at the Screening Visit.
8. No physical limitations that would prevent the subject from undergoing motor function
outcome measures throughout the study.
9. Females of childbearing potential must have a negative pregnancy test at Screening and agree
to use at least 1 acceptable method of contraception throughout the trial and for 20 weeks
after the last dose of study drug. Female subjects who are expected to have reached
reproductive maturity by the end of the trial must agree to adhere to trial-specific
contraception requirements.
Exclusion Criteria
Subjects meeting any of the following criteria were not eligible for the study:
1. Received onasemnogene abeparvovec-xioi at any time.
2. Previous treatment with apitegromab.

32
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

3. Prior history of severe hypersensitivity reaction or intolerance to SMN upregulator therapies,
or prior history of a hypersensitivity reaction to a monoclonal antibody or recombinant
protein bearing an Fc domain, apitegromab, or excipients of apitegromab.
4. Require invasive ventilation or tracheostomy.
5. Nutritional status that was not stable or the past 6 months and is not anticipated to be stable
throughout the trial or medical necessity for a gastric/nasogastric feeding tube, where the
majority of feeds are given by this route.
6. Major orthopedic or other interventional procedure, including spine or hip surgery,
considered to have the potential to substantially limit the ability of the subject to be evaluated
on any motor function outcome measures, within 6 months before Screening or anticipated
during the trial.
7. Treatment with other investigational drugs in a clinical trial within 3 months or 5 half-lives,
whichever is longer, before Screening.
8. Use of valproic acid or hydroxyurea within 90 days before Screening.
9. Use of therapies with potentially significant muscle effects (e.g., androgens, insulin-like
growth factor, growth hormone, systemic beta-agonist, botulinum toxin, or muscle relaxants
or muscle-enhancing supplements) or potentially significant neuromuscular effects (e.g.,
acetylcholinesterase inhibitors) other than approved SMN upregulator therapy within 60 days
before Screening.
10. Use of systemic corticosteroids within 60 days before Screening. Inhaled or topical steroids
are allowed.
11. Any acute or comorbid condition interfering with the well-being of the subject within 7 days
before Screening, including active systemic infection, the need for acute treatment, or
inpatient observation due to any reason.
12. Severe contractures or scoliosis (National Cancer Institute Common Terminology Criteria for
Adverse Events grade 3) at Screening. Based on clinical judgement, any contractures or
scoliosis present must be stable over the past 6 months, anticipated to be stable throughout
the trial, and not prevent the subject from being evaluated on any motor function outcome
measures throughout the trial.
13. Use of chronic daytime noninvasive ventilatory support for >16 hours daily in the 2 weeks
before dosing, or anticipated to regularly receive such daytime ventilator support chronically
throughout the trial.
14. Pregnant or breastfeeding.
15. Any other condition or clinically significant laboratory result or electrocardiogram (ECG)
value that, in the opinion of the Investigator, may compromise safety or compliance, would
preclude the subject from successful completion of the study, or interfere with the
interpretation of the results.

Multiplicity Adjustment
The 2 comparisons for the primary analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint were evaluated
simultaneously using the Hochberg procedure. If both comparisons achieved statistical
significance for the primary endpoint, the hierarchical testing approach would continue to
secondary endpoints in the order listed above.
Sample Size
A sample size of 50 subjects each in the apitegromab 20 mg/kg group and the placebo group
from MEP would yield at least 80% power to detect a mean (± standard deviation) difference of
3±5 points between the apitegromab 20 mg/kg group and the placebo group in the change from
Baseline in the HFMSE total score, at a two-sided alpha (α) level of 0.05 and assuming a drop
off rate of 5% after randomization.
With 50 subjects in the apitegromab 10 mg/kg group, a sample size of 100 subjects in the
apitegromab combined dose (10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg combined) group and 50 subjects in the
placebo group would yield at least 90% power to detect a mean (± standard deviation) difference
of 3±5 points between the apitegromab combined dose group and the placebo group.
With the Hochberg procedure (Hochberg 1988) to control for multiplicity of the 2 hypotheses,
the power to reject at least 1 of the hypotheses is approximately 90%.
Analysis Population
The modified intent-to-treat set is the main efficacy set for the primary analysis, consisting of all
randomized subjects in the MEP who received at least 1 dose of study drug and had at least one
postbaseline evaluable HFMSE assessment. In this study, all randomized subjects received at
least one dose of study drug and had at least one postbaseline evaluable HFMSE assessment.
EXP set is defined as all randomized subjects in the EXP who received at least one dose of study
drug.
Full analysis set (FAS) set is defined as all randomized subjects who received at least one dose
of study drug. FAS includes both MEP and EXP population.
Primary Analysis
The primary analysis for the main and supportive estimands is performed using a restricted
maximum likelihood model based mixed-effects model for repeated measures (MMRM) model
to analyze the change from Baseline in HFMSE total score at 12 months. The model includes the
fixed effects of treatment, visit, treatment-by-visit interaction, baseline HFMSE total score,
baseline HFMSE total score-by-time interaction, type of SMN2 therapy at randomization (i.e.,
nusinersen or risdiplam), and age at initiation of SMN2 therapy (≥5 and <5 years). An
unstructured covariance structure is used to model the within-patient variability. If the model
35
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
with the unstructured covariance structure fails to converge, the heterogeneous first-order
autoregression, AR(1), covariance structure are to be used. The Kenward-Roger method is used
to estimate the degrees of freedom.
Handling of Intercurrent Events
In the study, intercurrent events (ICEs) include


Premature stopping of study treatment due to adverse event (AE)



Premature stopping of the study treatment due to death



Premature stopping of study treatment due to reasons other than AE and death



Missed 3 or more consecutive doses impacted by the coronavirus disease of 2019 (COVID19)



Missed 3 or more consecutive doses other than impact by COVID-19



Had scoliosis/spinal surgery



Stopping receiving SMN2 therapy for longer than 6 months of nusinersen or 10 days of
risdiplam without switching to another SMN2 therapy



SMN2 therapy switched during the study

Only three types of ICEs occurred in seven subjects in this study: scoliosis/spinal surgery,
premature stopping of study treatment due to reasons other than AE and death, and SMN2
therapy stopping/switching. For the primary analysis, these ICEs were handled using the
treatment policy strategy.
Supportive/Sensitivity Analyses
For supportive estimand, the treatment policy strategy was used, except that ICEs of
scoliosis/spinal surgery were handled using hypothetical strategy, and values after such ICEs
were not used.
A sensitivity analysis was conducted by an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model adjusting
for treatment, type of SMN2 therapy at randomization (i.e., nusinersen or risdiplam), age at
initiation of SMN2 therapy (≥5 and <5 years), and baseline HFMSE total score. The sensitivity
analysis was performed using the imputed datasets assuming missing not at random for missing
data after ICEs premature stopping of study treatment due to reasons other than AE and death,
and SMN2 therapy stopping/switching. The Pattern-Mixture Model approach was implemented
to create a control-based pattern imputation. The missing values after scoliosis/spinal surgery
were imputed using probabilistic sampling from the lowest 10% values within in each treatment
group per visit. The results were combined based on Rubin’s rules.
If more than 5% of the MEP (>7 subjects in the MEP) had ICEs, an additional sensitivity
analysis was to be conducted by applying a tipping-point analysis to evaluate the robustness of
the estimates for the main estimand.

6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, SAPPHIRE
Study execution, protocol adherence, and data integrity appear adequate. The data generated are
interpretable. The Applicant attested to good clinical practices such as human subject protection,
institutional review board compliance, and clinical investigator obligations including financial
disclosures as defined in the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) scientific guideline
E6(R3) Guideline for Good Clinical Practice (July 2025), and in accordance with the ethical
principles that have their origin in the Declaration of Helsinki.
Disposition of Subjects
A total of 184 subjects were screened for the MEP, and 156 were randomized into either placebo
(n=50), apitegromab 10 mg/kg (n=53), or apitegromab 20 mg/kg (n=53). All randomized
subjects were exposed to at least one dose of study treatment and were included in the safety
analysis set. Two subjects discontinued study drug (both in the 20 mg/kg apitegromab group).

Protocol Deviations
There were 22 major protocol deviations reported in 19 (10.1%) subjects; the number of
deviations was similar between the apitegromab (9.4%) and placebo (11.7%) arms. The most
commonly reported major protocol deviation was “study procedure” (15/22 of the deviation
events; these included missing ECGs or missing motor function assessments at Visit 1 or Visit
14). Other deviations included randomization (n=3, due to incorrect stratification for the age of
initiation of SMN2 therapy), informed consent (n=2, due to delayed assent/consent), and
investigation product (n=2, both involving subjects receiving placebo; one deviation was due to
the study drug infusion volume and the second was due to temperature excursion in the study
drug storage location).
Overall, these protocol deviations did not affect the study integrity, efficacy, or safety
conclusions.
Efficacy Results - Primary Endpoint
The primary endpoint for SAPPHIRE was the change from baseline to Month 12 in the HFMSE
total score in the MEP, analyzed in the pooled apitegromab 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg dose groups
and in the apitegromab 20 mg/kg dose group alone. With an alpha of 0.05 for the two
comparisons evaluated by Hochberg procedure, statistical significance is achieved for the
primary endpoint based on the comparison of pooled apitegromab 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg versus
placebo with a p-value ≤0.025. In the MEP, the least squares (LS) mean difference (standard
error [SE]) in HFMSE change from baseline was 1.8 points (0.76; p=0.0192) for the apitegromab
10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg pooled dose versus placebo, favoring apitegromab.
The LS mean difference (SE) in HFMSE change from baseline was 1.4 (0.88; p=0.1149) when
the 20 mg/kg group was compared to placebo, which showed a numerical treatment benefit but
was not statistically significant. Because the comparison of 20 mg/kg versus placebo had a pvalue >0.05, all subsequent endpoint analyses from the hierarchical testing strategy were
considered exploratory. Note that the primary analysis did not include a planned comparison
between the 10 mg/kg group and placebo on the HFMSE endpoint. Assessment of the primary
outcome measure in the 10 mg/kg group was included as a secondary endpoint. Since the
secondary endpoints could not be formally tested unless both the primary endpoints were
statistically significant, this comparison of the 10 mg/kg group was not statistically significant
39
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
using the multiple comparisons procedure that was planned. However, we note that the LS mean
difference (SE) in HFMSE change from baseline was 2.2 (0.87; nominal p=0.0121) for the
apitegromab 10 mg/kg versus placebo comparison, which was nominally statistically significant.

Handling of ICEs and Missing Data
Overall, seven subjects in the MEP experienced ICEs. As fewer than 5% of subjects in the MEP
experienced ICEs, the tipping-point sensitivity analysis was not to be conducted.
41
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
A supportive estimand was specified to use hypothetical strategy for ICEs of scoliosis/spinal
surgery. Data after such ICEs were set to missing. Two analyses were conducted: an MMRM
assuming missing at random, and an ANCOVA model that uses probabilistic sampling from the
lowest 10% values within in each treatment group per visit to impute data following such ICEs.
In the MEP, four subjects underwent such surgeries: two in the placebo group, and one each in
the apitegromab 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg groups. The worst scores of each group were from these
subjects. The analyses using the hypothetical strategy essentially replaced the worst observed
scores with better imputed or assumed values, which may not be appropriate. The results based
on the hypothetical strategy were slightly better than the primary analysis (results not shown in
table), although these analyses may not be meaningful given the situation discussed above.
Only one subject with an ICE had missing data. This subject withdrew from the study due to
parent/guardian choice. The prespecified sensitivity analysis that imputed missing data after
ICEs assuming missing not at random had minimal impact on the analysis results due to the
small amount of missing data (results not shown in table).
In summary, the primary analysis results are robust with respect to the handling of ICEs and
missing data.
Discussion on Numerical Difference in Effect Size Between the Two Dose Groups
The Applicant attributes the numerical difference in effect size between the two dose groups to
an imbalance in the number of younger subjects aged 2 to 3 years (4 in the 10 mg/kg group
versus 2 in the 20 mg/kg group). These younger subjects had a relatively shorter duration of prior
SMN2 therapy, the effects of which tend to plateau after 3 to 4 years of treatment. As a result, a
larger effect size was observed in younger subjects and in the 10 mg/kg dose group, which
included more of these younger subjects. Excluding the nine subjects aged 2 to 3 years results in
a more similar treatment effect between apitegromab 10 mg/kg, apitegromab 20 mg/kg, and the
pooled apitegromab group (Table 13).
Only two out of the nine subjects aged 2 to 3 years had a substantial change from baseline in
(b) (4) had a change from baseline of 16 at Week 52, and Subject
HFMSE score: Subject #
(b)
(4)
#
had a change from baseline of 18 at Week 52. Both subjects were in the 10 mg/kg dose
group and may have driven the nominally significant result in this group (i.e., the primary
analysis excluding these two subjects resulted in a LS mean difference of 1.6 and a larger
nominal p-value of 0.0483 for the 10 mg/kg dose group, similar to the LS mean difference and pvalue shown in 4- to 12-year-olds in Table 13).

Sample Size for SAPPHIRE
The sample size for SAPPHIRE was calculated based on the assumed mean (± standard
deviation) difference of 3±5 points between the apitegromab and the placebo group in the change
from Baseline in HFMSE. However, the observed mean difference between apitegromab
20 mg/kg group and the placebo group was only half of the assumed 3-point difference, and the
observed standard deviation remained approximately 5. The sample size for MEP population did
not provide sufficient power to detect a treatment effect of 1.5 points.
When combining the MEP and the EXP populations (i.e., FAS population), a nominal statistical
significance was achieved for a similar treatment effect but with reduced variance in the
apitegromab 20 mg/kg group (Table 14). The Applicant’s result for the FAS population, as
presented in the clinical study report, is different from the analysis below as the Applicant’s
analysis did not adjust for age group (2 to 12 years for MEP and 13 to 21 years for EXP) in the
model. This is inappropriate, given the randomization ratios were different between the age
groups.

Discussion on Testing Procedure
The two comparisons for the primary analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint, one for the
combined dose group and one for the 20 mg/kg group, were evaluated simultaneously using the
Hochberg procedure. If both comparisons demonstrated statistical significance for the primary
endpoint, hierarchical testing would proceed to the secondary endpoints in the predefined order.
This testing strategy assumes that the combined dose group is clinically relevant.
If the combined dose was not relevant and played no role in the testing strategy, then a Hochberg
procedure testing the high-dose and low-dose groups simultaneously would be more appropriate.
Under that approach, the low dose would reach statistical significance, but not the high dose.
Other procedures, such as a fixed-sequence testing strategy starting with the high dose followed
by the low dose, could also be considered. In either scenario, the secondary endpoints would not
be formally tested unless both dose groups demonstrated statistical significance on the primary
endpoint.
Discussion of Results
In SAPPHIRE, the Applicant enrolled nonambulatory subjects with SMA and randomized them
to receive apitegromab 10 mg/kg or 20 mg/kg, or placebo. The primary efficacy analysis
population (MEP) consisted of the subset of subjects 2 to 12 years of age. The primary endpoint,
mean change from baseline in the HFMSE at Month 12, and the key secondary endpoints, mean
change from baseline in the RULM and the WHO Motor Development Milestones at Month 12,
were evaluated in the MEP. In the primary endpoint analysis of the MEP there was a statistically
significant difference favoring the pooled apitegromab 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg dose groups. The
difference between the apitegromab 20 mg/kg dose group and placebo did not achieve statistical
significance (though numerical trends towards improvement were seen in this group) and
therefore all other analyses are considered exploratory. In the analysis of the primary endpoint in
the apitegromab 10 mg/kg dose group compared to placebo (a prespecified secondary endpoint),
there was a nominally statistically significant difference favoring the 10 mg/kg apitegromab dose
group. Numerical trends towards improvement were also seen in both the 10 mg/kg and
20 mg/kg apitegromab groups analyzed separately compared to placebo for both of the key
secondary endpoints. The proportion of clinical responders (defined as improvement in the
HFMSE ≥3 points by Month 12) was numerically higher in all of apitegromab dose groups
44
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
compared to those in the placebo group and met nominal statistical significance in the pooled
dose group and in the 10 mg/kg dose group.
The EXP also demonstrated numerical trends in improvement in the 20 mg/kg dose group over
placebo, though it did not meet nominal statistical significance. See Section 6.3 for a discussion
of the adequacy of the results of the primary endpoint to support the efficacy of the proposed
marketing dose of 10 mg/kg.
Conclusion
Overall, the primary and secondary efficacy results of SAPPHIRE indicate an improvement in
motor function in a greater number of subjects with SMA treated with apitegromab compared to
those treated with placebo, supporting the clinical efficacy of apitegromab in patients on
background SMN2 targeted therapies. As patients who had received onasemnogene were
excluded from participation in SAPPHIRE, there are no data to support efficacy in these patients.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Efficacy of the Proposed Marketing Dose
Issue
Evaluate whether the evidence provided by the Applicant supports the substantial evidence of
effectiveness for apitegromab as an adjunctive therapy for the treatment of SMA in pediatric and
adult patients 2 years of age and older who are currently on a SMN2-targeted treatment, and
whether the proposed marketing dose of apitegromab 10 mg/kg administered Q4W is the optimal
dosing regimen.
6.3.1.1. Background of the Issue
The original protocol (dated September 29, 2021) for SAPPHIRE had the following primary
hypothesis of interest: whether apitegromab 20 mg/kg has superior efficacy compared with
placebo in subjects ages 2 to 12 years of age treated with background SMN2 targeted therapies.
If superiority is demonstrated with the 20 mg/kg group, the hypothesis of whether apitegromab
10 mg/kg has superior efficacy compared with placebo in subjects ages 2 through 12 years of age
treated with background SMN therapies would be tested to determine the biologically optimal
dose.
In a type C meeting (minutes dated November 30, 2023), the Sponsor proposed changes to the
statistical analysis plan for the ongoing phase 3 study SAPPHIRE, where the primary hypothesis
of interest now was whether apitegromab 10 mg/kg or 20 mg/kg (pooled dose group) has
superior efficacy compared to placebo in subjects ages 2 to 12 on background SMN2 targeted
therapies. The Division expressed the following concerns with this revised approach:
Unless it was a preplanned adaptation, it is problematic to change the primary hypothesis
for an ongoing study. Late changes may raise questions about study integrity. We do not
recommend pooling dose levels for the purpose of the primary analysis method, as it may
lead to significant interpretability issues for efficacy and safety. For example, if the

45
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
pooled dose-group hypothesis test shows the pooled group to be superior to placebo,
potentially problematic scenarios may arise for the individual dose-group test results: a)
neither dose tests individually superior to placebo; b) 10 mg/kg demonstrates superiority
to placebo but the 20 mg/kg dose does not. Additionally, it may be difficult to identify a
safe and effective dose for labeling if an unexpected safety signal arises at the higher
dose (i.e., 20 mg/kg), but the efficacy of the 10 mg/kg dose cannot be determined from
the pooled dose analysis.4

Subsequently, the results of the SAPPHIRE provided results consistent with that described as a
“potentially problematic scenario” above: the pooled dose-group hypothesis test showed the
pooled group to be superior to placebo, the 10 mg/kg demonstrated superiority to placebo (with
nominal significance) but the 20 mg/kg dose did not demonstrate superiority.
In the pre-BLA meeting (minutes dated January 6, 2025), the Division stated the following:
As we previously noted, pooling of dose levels for the purpose of the primary analysis
method may lead to significant interpretability issues for efficacy and safety. We note
that the pooled dose-group hypothesis test shows the pooled group to be superior to
placebo; however, the 10 mg/kg dose was superior to placebo, but the 20 mg/kg dose was
not. This clinical situation where “low dose wins” and “high dose loses” is difficult to
interpret and will be a significant review issue.5

Additionally, TOPAZ, the phase 2 trial (discussed in Section 15), was not a placebo-controlled
trial and evaluated doses of 2 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg Q4W. In the ambulatory cohorts receiving
20 mg/kg, no improvement in the primary endpoint was seen by Month 12, whereas the
responses were varied in the nonambulatory cohorts, and in subjects ages 2 and older on
nusinersen background therapy (Cohort 3) both 2 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg demonstrated
improvements in the primary endpoint by Month 12. As such, several analyses were conducted
to evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed marketing dose of 10 mg/kg Q4W to assist with the
interpretation of this data, as discussed below.
6.3.1.2. Assessment of HFMSE Results in SAPPHIRE
The primary endpoint for SAPPHIRE was the change from baseline to Month 12 in the HFMSE
total score in the MEP. In the primary endpoint analysis in the pooled apitegromab group, there
was a statistically significant difference of 1.8 points favoring apitegromab (0.6-point LS mean
in the pooled apitegromab group and -1.2-point LS mean in the placebo group; p=0.0192). For
the primary endpoint analysis of the apitegromab 20 mg/kg group, there was a numerical
difference of 1.4 points favoring apitegromab in the HFMSE change from baseline to Month 12,
but this difference was not statistically significant (p=0.1149). Therefore, based on the Hochberg
procedure utilized, all subsequent analyses were considered exploratory. However, the LS mean
difference in HFMSE change from baseline was 2.2 points (nominal p =0.0121) when the 10
mg/kg group was compared to placebo.

The numerical difference in effect size between the 20 mg/kg dose group and the 10 mg/kg dose
group may be due to a few subjects in the 10 mg/kg dose group who showed higher treatment
response and small overall numbers. There was an imbalance in the number of younger subjects
aged 2 to 3 years (4 in the 10 mg/kg group versus 2 in the 20 mg/kg group) who have a relatively
shorter duration of prior SMN2 therapy, the effects of which tend to plateau after 3 to 4 years of
treatment. Thus, it is not clear what contribution the underlying SMN2 therapy may have had on
the larger effect size that was observed in younger subjects, or if that was driving the larger
effect size in the 10 mg/kg dose group, which included more of these younger subjects. When
subjects aged 2 to 3 years were excluded from the primary analysis set, there was a similar
treatment effect between apitegromab 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg (Table 13), and the pooled
apitegromab and 10 mg/kg arm were both still nominally significant compared to placebo. This
finding is supported by analysis of latent myostatin as discussed in Section 6.3.1.4 below.

cumulative density function (See Figure 4 below) for the change in HFMSE scores in both
the main efficacy population (MEP) and exploratory efficacy population (EXP) in
SAPPHIRE. The cumulative density function plot shows separation between placebo and the
two dose groups, suggestive of a treatment response. Additionally, there appears to be a
marginally higher trend in treatment response for 10 mg/kg compared to 20 mg/kg.

6.3.1.3. Assessment of Primary Endpoint Results of TOPAZ
TOPAZ was a phase 2, active-controlled study that assessed apitegromab in ambulatory and
nonambulatory subjects with later-onset SMA, who were either on concomitant nusinersen or
were SMN-therapy naïve. TOPAZ had 3 separate cohorts:


Cohort 1 consisted of ambulatory subjects with Type 3 SMA, 5 to 21 years of age who
received either 20 mg/kg apitegromab alone Q4W or 20 mg/kg apitegromab + nusinersen
Q4W.



Cohort 2 consisted of nonambulatory subjects with Type 2 or 3 SMA 5 to 21 years of age
already on nusinersen, all of whom received 20 mg/kg apitegromab Q4W.



Cohort 3 consisted of nonambulatory subjects with Type 2 SMA aged 2 years and older
already receiving nusinersen. Subjects were randomized to either 20 mg/kg apitegromab
Q4W or 2 mg/kg apitegromab Q4W; all subjects in Cohort 3 remained on nusinersen.

In Cohort 1, the primary efficacy endpoint was the mean change from baseline in the Revised
Hammersmith Scale (RHS) total score at Month 12. Cohort 1 demonstrated no change (stability)
in their RHS total scores by Month 12, regardless of whether subjects were on concomitant
nusinersen. In Cohorts 2 and 3, the primary efficacy endpoint was the mean change from
baseline in the HFMSE total score at Month 12. Cohort 2 experienced a minimal mean score
increase in the HFMSE by Month 12 (0.6 points). In Cohort 3, subjects in both dose groups had
increases in their mean HFMSE scores by Month 12 (5.3 points and 7.1 points in the 2 mg/kg
and 20 mg/kg dose groups, respectively).
The data provided from TOPAZ, including the observed dose response between 2 mg/kg and 20
mg/kg in Cohort 3, suggests that apitegromab may support stabilization of motor function over
time, particularly in subjects with adjunctive nusinersen, and especially in more severely affected
and nonambulatory younger subjects.
There are several limitations to this study that impact the conclusions that may be drawn and
limited the use of TOPAZ as confirmatory evidence. TOPAZ was not a placebo-controlled study
and included multiple cohorts, each of which had its own primary efficacy analysis and enrolled
a small number of subjects. The dose proposed for labeling was not assessed in TOPAZ and the
study populations were somewhat different (some subjects were ambulatory, and none were on
concomitant risdiplam). Given the lack of a concurrent placebo group, it is not clear what impact
concomitant nusinersen therapy had on the outcomes. This is especially true for Cohort 3, which
included the youngest subjects who had been on nusinersen for the shortest time and may not
have experienced the reported plateau in treatment effect that is reported with SMN therapies.
6.3.1.4. Assessment of Latent Myostatin in SAPPHIRE and
TOPAZ
The review team conducted several exploratory analyses, which taken together contribute to
confirmatory evidence of effectiveness of apitegromab 10 mg/kg Q4W in the treatment of
patients with SMA. Apitegromab is hypothesized to selectively bind to promyostatin and latent
myostatin and inhibit the activation of myostatin, blocking myostatin signaling and preventing
the upregulation of muscle-atrophy-related genes and loss of skeletal muscle mass. The
Applicant measured latent myostatin concentrations in the serum and indicated that promyostatin
49
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
concentrations are localized to skeletal muscle tissue and not detectable in serum. The Applicant
noted that apitegromab, though hypothesized to bind to both pro- and latent-forms of myostatin,
only inhibits the tolloid-like protease cleavage of latent to mature myostatin and does not inhibit
the initial step of cleavage of pro- to latent myostatin. The bioanalytical method validation data
suggest that the combination of capture and detection reagents support selective and reliable
measurement of latent myostatin concentrations in serum (refer to Bioanalytical Section 14.3).
Therefore, measurements of serum latent myostatin concentrations may be used to reflect target
engagement of apitegromab.
The key findings from these exploratory analyses of latent myostatin are summarized below:
1. Observed increase in serum latent myostatin concentrations reflect target engagement of
apitegromab and support the biological plausibility that the drug is having its intended
pharmacodynamic effect on myostatin.
2. The longitudinal changes (relative to baseline) in serum latent myostatin concentrations were
comparable between 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg (Panel A), and there is no apparent doseresponse in serum latent myostatin levels at end of Year 1 (Panel B), suggesting similar
target engagement at both the dose levels (Figure 6 and Figure 7). The longitudinal changes
(relative to baseline) in HFMSE scores, and the dose-response in HFMSE scores at the end of
Year 1 in the main efficacy population (2 to 12 years age) in SAPPHIRE seems to suggest
numerically higher treatment response for 10 mg/kg group compared to 20 mg/kg. Overall,
these findings indicate that the observed statistical differences in the treatment response
(HFMSE score) noted above are unlikely to be due to differences in target engagement, i.e.,
serum latent myostatin levels.

6.3.1.5. Conclusion
In summary, there is substantial evidence of effectiveness for apitegromab as an adjunctive
therapy in treatment of patients with SMA 2 years of age and older receiving SMN2 therapy, at a
dose of 10 mg/kg Q4weeks. This evidence is comprised of a single, positive, adequate, and wellcontrolled trial of apitegromab in subjects with SMA 2 years of age and older in which there was
evidence of a clinically meaningful benefit on motor function on an acceptable primary efficacy
endpoint (mean change from baseline in the HFMSE) for the pooled 10+20 mg/kg apitegromab
group, along with nominally significant results on the same endpoint in a comparison of the 10
mg/kg apitegromab group to placebo. The overall persuasiveness of the findings in SAPPHIRE
is supported by similar magnitude change from baseline in HFMSE scores in the exploratory
efficacy population and consistency of numerical trends on all of the key secondary endpoints,
including nominal significance on the secondary endpoint of proportion of subjects with ≥3 point
change in HFMSE. The findings were also robust to missing data, and there were still nominally
significant findings with similar magnitude of effect on the primary endpoint when the 2- to 3year-old subjects, who were driving the largest treatment response, were removed from the
analysis.
There are statistical limitations to this small study that limit its ability to serve as a single study
alone to support the effectiveness in treatment of SMA. However, the findings of longitudinal
changes in pharmacokinetics and latent myostatin concentrations in SAPPHIRE demonstrate
evidence of target engagement which provides confirmatory evidence of a pharmacodynamic
56
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
effect on the muscle. Additional confirmatory evidence also comes from the findings in the
Phase 2 study, TOPAZ, which demonstrated (1) dose-proportional increases (~10-fold) in PK
exposures from 2 to 20 mg/kg but only 2.5-fold increase in latent myostatin concentrations and
1.4-fold increase in HFMSE scores at end of year 1, suggesting a plateau effect between 2 and 20
mg/kg, and (2) increases in PK and latent myostatin concentrations when subjects switched from
2 to 20 mg/kg at the end of year 1.
Further, there was a question of the optimal dosing regimen given the mixed findings in the 20
mg/kg dose and 10 mg/kg dose analyses in SAPPHIRE. The evidence demonstrates that the dose
proposed for marketing, apitegromab 10 mg/kg administered Q4W, is the optimal dosing
regimen. This is based on comparable longitudinal changes in the target engagement biomarker
latent myostatin concentrations between 10 mg/kg Q4W and 20 mg/kg Q4W regimens, and a
lack of a clear dose-response relationship in the latent myostatin concentrations at the end of year
1 in MEP. The trends observed in the change from baseline in HFMSE scores and latent
myostatin levels following 20 mg/kg Q4W in EXP were consistent with the same trends
following 10 mg/kg Q4Wand 20 mg/kg Q4W in MEP, suggesting 20 mg/kg Q4W is unlikely to
confer additional treatment benefit over the 10 mg/kg regimen as an adjunctive therapy in
patients with SMA 2 years of age and older.
Overall, the findings of the positive single, adequate and well-controlled study plus the
confirmatory evidence of findings on latent myostatin, demonstrating target engagement and
pharmacodynamic evidence of the mechanism of action, are sufficient to demonstrate substantial
evidence of effectiveness of apitegromab 10 mg/kg as add-on treatment to SMN2 therapy in
adults and pediatric subjects 2 years of age and older.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
In nonclinical studies that are considered adequate to assess the safety of SRK-015, bone and
reproductive and developmental toxicity were identified as potential clinical risks of SRK-015
administration.
Bone toxicity: In a 12-week intravenous (IV) toxicity study in rat (0, 10, 30, 100, or 300
mg/kg/week), what was described as “fissure at the femoral head physis” was observed at all
doses in the special histopathology examinations of the femur performed at the end of the dosing
and recovery periods. At the end of the dosing period, fissure at the physis was observed in 3/10,
2/10, 4/10 and 1/10 males at the low dose (LD), low-mid dose (LMD), high-mid dose (HMD),
and high dose (HD), respectively, and in 1/10, 2/10, and 1/10 females in the control (C), LD, and
LMD groups, respectively. The epiphysis was partially or completely absent in all affected
animals from SRK-015-treated groups but was present in the single affected C female. At the
recovery sacrifice, physeal fissure was observed in only 3 animals: 1/5 LD males, 1/5 HD males,
and 1/5 HMD females, and the epiphysis was partially or completely absent in all three affected
animals. Physeal fissure correlated with missing femoral head observed on computed
tomography (CT) imaging.
57
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
In affected animals at both sacrifice times, fissure was associated with degenerative (resorption)
and reactive (fibrosis, woven bone, hyperplastic osteoclasts) changes at the femoral neck and
proximal metaphysis. According to the study pathologist: “The changes observed at the femoral
head in this study, in particular those with bone resorption, fibrosis, increased woven bone and
osteoclast at the femoral neck and proximal metaphysis, were similar to femoral head
osteonecrosis described in laboratory animals other than SD rats.” The pathologist compared the
finding to corticosteroid-induced osteonecrosis that has been reported clinically and
experimentally (Boss and Misselevich 2003). While noting that a definitive cause of the fissure
could not be identified, the pathologist concluded:
However, in light of the presence of other changes in the metaphysis in 5 of 99 animals in
the main study and in 3 of 50 animals in the recovery study (resorption, fibrosis,
increased woven bone and/or osteoclast hyperplasia in the metaphysis), the physeal
fissure was likely a real antemortem change. In addition, based on what is known of
femoral head necrosis in rats, and given the higher incidence of physeal fissure at 10 and
100 mg/kg/week in males associated with metaphyseal changes and absence of such
changes in the control males and absence of reactive bone change in the one control
female with physeal fissure, a treatment-related effect cannot be ruled out.

No clear effects on bone mineral density (BMD) were observed; however, partial or complete
missing femoral head epiphysis were noted in the qualitative evaluation of CT images only in
treated animals and these animals were excluded from BMD analysis. As described in the CT
imaging report, one LMD female had clinical observations of limited use of the right hind leg on
Day 86 of the dosing phase and a macroscopic observation of a fractured proximal right femur at
necropsy. A CT image of the right femur from a LD male with missing femoral head epiphysis
showed bone loss at the proximal femur metaphysis, and one HMD male also showed bone loss
at the femoral head epiphysis and proximal femur metaphysis. The HD male showed bone loss at
the distal metaphysis along with increased cortical porosity at the distal metaphysis and distal
diaphysis. The total femur BMD value for this animal was lower compared to other HD animals.
The finding of missing femoral head epiphysis was also seen in SRK-015-treated recovery
animals. These animals showed bone loss at the proximal femur metaphysis, including at the
femoral neck region. The report concluded:
The qualitative observation for missing epiphysis of the femoral head from the right
femur that was observed in animals administered ≥10 mg/kg/week SRK-015 may be test
article-related; however, further investigation via histopathology or similar techniques
would be needed to qualify this finding along with the observed bone loss in a few of the
femurs via the 2D and 3D CT images.

In the 26-week IV toxicity study in rat (0, 30, 100, or 300 mg/kg/week), the histopathological
examination of the femur revealed necrosis of the femoral head at the terminal (1/10 C, 3/10 LD,
1/10 MD, and 1/10 HD males and 1/10 HD females) and recovery (1/5 C, 1/5 LD, 1/5 MD, and
1/5 HD) sacrifices. The femoral head findings were not considered drug-related by the study
pathologist, based on the similar nature and severity of the necrosis and associated fibrosis or
bone/cartilage intraarticular debris in all dose groups, including controls. However, no
explanation for these findings was given. Changes in the femoral head/neck were also observed
by in vivo radiography in animals from all groups, including controls, at the end of dosing and
recovery. However, there was a dose-related increase in the incidence and severity of
radiographic findings, e.g., the numbers of normal femur heads (score =0) at the Week 27
58
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
evaluation were 48/60, 34/58, 10/60, and 0/60 in the C, LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively.
According to the study report, the lack of correlating drug-related microscopic findings in the
femur head/neck indicated that the alterations observed radiographically were likely incidental,
since histopathology is considered more sensitive than radiography. But the clear dose-related
incidence and severity of the radiography findings suggest otherwise.
Literature reports indicate that elevated myostatin levels lead to decreased BMD and that
myostatin inhibition improves BMD and increases both muscle and bone mass. Experimental
data support indirect positive effects on bone strength attributed to increased muscle mass as well
as direct negative effects of myostatin on bone mass through reduced bone formation and
increased resorption (Suh and Lee 2020). Since active myostatin is a negative regulator of
muscle and bone, decreasing myostatin would be expected to increase muscle and bone mass. In
the rat studies, muscle mass and weights were increased as expected, but in the assessments of
bone, which were extensive, only detrimental effects were observed, as described above. It has
been reported that myostatin inhibitors that also inhibit GDF11, such as follistatin, can induce
adverse effects on bone through GDF11 inhibition (Suh et al. 2020). However, no detectable
binding of SRK-015 to pro- or mature GDF11 was observed. It is not clear whether the
observation of increased fractures in apitegromab-treated subjects in the clinical trials (see
clinical review) is consistent with the rat findings. The radiology report from the 12-week study
described a “slipped out epiphysis of the femoral head” in the CT image of the femur from a
HMD male rat. This terminology, as well as the term fissure, is used to describe a hip condition
seen in children and adolescents (slipped capital femoral epiphysis) in which the femoral head
slips backward off its growth plate, misaligning the hip joint (Johns et al. 2023). Slipped capital
femoral epiphysis usually develops during periods of rapid growth, shortly after the onset of
puberty and is thought to be the result of an increased mechanical load, a weakened physis, or
both (Witbreuk et al. 2013). It seems possible that the increased muscle mass resulting from the
pharmacological action of SRK-015 and/or possible endocrine disruption (Ongaro et al. 2025)
could contribute to this effect in rats.
Reproductive and developmental toxicity: In the fertility and early embryonic development study
in rat, decreased sperm concentration, estrous cycle disruption, decreased male and/or female
mating and fertility indices, and increased postimplantation loss were observed. Mating and
fertility indices were decreased slightly compared to C at all doses in males and the female
fertility index was decreased at the LD and HD. Although the group differences were small and
non-dose-related, it appeared that effects on sperm concentration and estrous cyclicity were
reflected in impaired fertility. Postimplantation loss was increased at all doses but not in a doserelated manner. Since the lack of a dose-response was often used in the study reports to dismiss
toxicity findings, it is important to note that the pharmacological effect of SRK-015 on
myostatin, as reflected in plasma levels of latent myostatin, was often not dose-related (e.g., Day
184 in the 26-week study), indicating saturation of the target at the doses evaluated as was also
seen clinically at doses ≥10 mg/kg. To the extent that findings are related to SRK-015’s
pharmacological activity, a dose-response may not be expected. Increased postimplantation loss
was also seen in the embryo-fetal development (EFD) studies in rat and rabbit; however,
variability and lack of dose-response in both species and confounding by possible drug
contamination in the rabbit study made it difficult to reach definite conclusions.
Effects on reproductive system development were also observed in the pre- and postnatal
development (PPND) and juvenile animal (JAS) studies in rat. In the PPND study, sexual
59
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
maturation was delayed at all doses in both sexes and mating and fertility indices were decreased
in HD male offspring. A dose-dependent increase in the maximum auditory startle response was
also observed in SRK-015-exposed offspring. In the 7-week JAS, with initiation of dosing at
postnatal day (PND) 21, there were no clearly SRK-015-related effects on mating and fertility
parameters when evaluated during the recovery period, i.e., naïve females were mated with
treated males and treated females were mated with naïve males. However, on C-section of
pregnant treated females, there was a decrease in corpora lutea and an increase in
preimplantation loss, with corresponding decreases in numbers of implantation sites and live
fetuses. There were no apparent effects on BMD or bone histopathology in this JAS. In a 3-week
rat JAS with initiation of dosing on PND 7, no effects on postnatal development were observed.
However, the assessment of possible bone effects consisted only of femur length measurement
and routine histopathology.
Literature describes a biological role for myostatin in reproductive function, including in the
regulation of follicular development, ovarian steroidogenesis, granule-cell proliferation, and
oocyte maturation (Wang et al. 2022). A study of the fertility of myostatin gene (MSTN)
knockout (MSTN−/−) pigs showed that although heterozygous MSTN ± sows displayed normal
reproductive capacity, their litter size was significantly lower than that of wild-type (WT) sows,
and the age at puberty was delayed in MSTN ± sows compared to WT sows (Han et al. 2021). In
addition, a recent publication describes a functional role for myostatin as an endocrine hormone
that directly promotes pituitary follicle-stimulating hormone synthesis and thereby ovarian
function in mice, suggesting that efforts to antagonize myostatin to increase muscle mass may
have unintended consequences on fertility (Ongaro et al. 2025). These publications suggest that
the adverse reproductive and developmental effects observed in the studies of SRK-015 are
consistent with a reduction in myostatin activity.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
This is a first-in-class drug and therefore the potential risks of this class (myostatin inhibitors)
are not fully known.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Not applicable. Apitegromab is a new molecular entity. It is not approved in the United States or
in any foreign market.

7.3.1. Adverse Events Identified in Postmarket
Experiences
Not applicable. Apitegromab is not approved in the United States or other regions of the world.

7.3.2. Expectations on Safety
The safety and effectiveness of apitegromab in women who are pregnant or breastfeeding have
not been adequately characterized in clinical trials. The risk of malignancy is unknown.

7.3.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
Not applicable.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
Clinical study data were independently analyzed with assistance provided by the clinical data
scientist support team. The BLA submission was well-organized. The submission quality with
respect to the Applicant’s clinical safety assessments was acceptable. No major data quality or
integrity issues were identified that would preclude a safety review of this BLA. There were no
major issues identified with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs. The Applicant’s
translations of verbatim terms to the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities version 26.1
preferred terms for the events reported in studies SAPPHIRE and ONYX were found to be
acceptable. The FDA analysis reviewed AE coding to assess the accuracy of the translation from
the verbatim to the preferred terms and found the translation adequate. Information submitted as
part of BLA 761463, as well as published information related to myostatin, SMA and other
relevant published literature, are discussed in this review. Data from the completed pivotal
double-blind, placebo-controlled study (Study SAPPHIRE) and the open-label extension study
ONYX formed the basis of clinical safety evaluation in adult and pediatric subjects with SMA.
Discussions on the safety data from SAPPHIRE and ONYX are found in Section 7.6.1 and
Section 7.6.2, respectively.
In Studies SAPPHIRE and ONYX, the treatment emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were
protocol-defined as any AE regardless of relatedness of the study drug that occurred or worsened
from the time a signed and dated informed consent form is obtained until completion of the
subject’s last study-related procedure, which may include contact for follow-up of safety. An AE
with a completely missing start date was assumed to be treatment emergent. The severity of AEs
was graded as mild, moderate, severe, life-threatening/disabling, or leading to death by the
investigator based on the National Cancer Institute Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse
Events grading scale, version 5.0 (NIH 2025).
In addition to queries for AEs, assessments of vital signs, physical and neurological examination,
and laboratory monitoring were performed in Studies SAPPHIRE and ONYX. Laboratory
monitoring included assessments of serum chemistry (including CPK and uric acid), hematology,
coagulation, urinalysis, liver function testing, lipids, pregnancy testing, ECGs, and antidrug
antibodies. The vital signs and laboratory safety assessments are discussed in Sections 7.6.1.8
and 7.6.1.6, respectively. The Assessment for antidrug antibodies is discussed in Section 5.2.
Study ONYX is discussed in Section 7.6.2. A summary of the schedule of safety assessments is
presented in Table 16 below.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The safety database is adequate for a comprehensive safety assessment of apitegromab for the
proposed indication, subject population, dosage regimen, and duration. The safety population is
representative of, and thus applicable to, the population of interest. The overall subject exposure
in the clinical development program was found to be adequate.
The initial data cutoff date for the BLA submission was June 3, 2024. At that time all but one
subject had completed SAPPHIRE and 185/188 subjects from SAPPHIRE had continued into
ONYX.
SAPPHIRE evaluated two efficacy populations, the MEP, which was defined as subjects 2 to 12
years of age, and an EXP, which included subjects 13 to 21 years of age. The overall number of
subjects exposed to apitegromab are summarized in Table 17 and Table 18 below for each
population.

7.6. Safety Results
7.6.1. Safety Results, SAPPHIRE
The safety results will be presented for both the MEP and EXP separately throughout the section.
While there were more serious and severe safety events in the MEP (and particularly in the
10 mg/kg dose group), the safety results are overall consistent amongst the MEP and EXP and
between both the 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg apitegromab dose groups. The discrepancy in the
frequency of serious and severe safety events may be attributed to the MEP enrolling younger
children with more severe disease who may be more vulnerable to serious adverse events such as
infections.
7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, SAPPHIRE
Table 19 and Table 20 show an overview of TEAEs observed in both the MEP and EXP during
the double-blind phase of SAPPHIRE.
In the MEP, there was a higher frequency of TEAEs in the 10 mg/kg dose group (96.2%) than in
the 20 mg/kg group (86.8%) or the placebo group (86%). There was also a higher frequency of
serious adverse events (SAEs) in both apitegromab dose groups (17% and 22.6% in the 10 mg/kg
and 20 mg/kg groups, respectively, versus 10% in placebo). There were no adverse events
leading to study drug discontinuation. The majority of TEAEs were mild-to-moderate.

7.6.1.2. Deaths, SAPPHIRE
There were no deaths during the double-blind period of SAPPHIRE in either the MEP or EXP.

severity/grade or relationship to investigational medication after the administration of study drug.
Serious adverse events defined as any untoward medical occurrence that at any dose results in death, is life-threatening, requires hospitalization or prolongation of existing
hospitalization, results in persistent incapacity or substantial disruption of the ability to conduct normal life functions, or is a congenital anomaly or birth defect.
Duration is 52 weeks of treatment.
Risk difference (with 95% confidence interval) is shown between total treatment and comparator.
Asterisk (*) indicates that 95% confidence interval excludes zero.
Abbreviations: N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number of subjects with adverse event; SAE, serious adverse event; SOC, system organ class

Respiratory comorbidities are common in patients with SMA, due to their underlying restrictive
lung disease, muscular weakness, weakened cough, and feeding related problems, which are
confounding factors in these cases (Lagae et al. 2024). However, it should be noted that the
SAEs of pneumonia and dyspnea occurred only in subjects treated with apitegromab, and not in
the placebo arm, and there was one SAE of acute respiratory failure in the placebo group (in a
subject with respiratory syncytial virus bronchiolitis); therefore, a causal role for apitegromab
cannot be definitively ruled out.

alternative cause for this event was found.

While no alternative cause was found for this subject’s immune thrombocytopenia, a causal role
for apitegromab is unlikely given (b) (6) normalization with and lack of repeat thrombocytopenia
while continuing treatment. Additionally, no other similar events have been identified.
In the EXP, no SAEs reported in the apitegromab treatment arm. One subject in the placebo
group experienced an SAE of scoliosis.
7.6.1.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment
Discontinuation, SAPPHIRE
No subjects discontinued SAPPHIRE due to adverse events in either the MEP or EXP.
There were two subjects who discontinued for reasons other than an adverse event. While not
directly linked to an adverse event, adverse events may have played a role in the decision to
discontinue study treatment, briefly described below.
(b) (6) . In brief, (b) (6) was a

treatment with study drug was discontinued due to the withdrawal of consent of the subject’s

Isembyld (apitegromab)
7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, SAPPHIRE
Overall, the Agency’s analyses of the laboratory value abnormalities for the double-blind period
of SAPPHIRE identified no clinically significant patterns or trends in the measured blood
chemistry or hematology parameters, except for CPK, which is discussed in Section 7.7.2.
7.6.1.6.1. MEP Laboratory Results, SAPPHIRE
Chemistry
No clinically significant chemistry laboratory abnormalities were noted for the MEP population
receiving apitegromab during the double-blind period of SAPPHIRE. See Section 7.7.2 for a
discussion of CPK.
Kidney Function
There were no clinically significant abnormalities in the MEP receiving apitegromab in
SAPPHIRE (Table 28). Subjects with severe or uncontrolled renal disease were not enrolled in
SAPPHIRE. A total of nine subjects (8.5%) in the MEP in both apitegromab dose groups had
estimated glomerular filtration rate (GFR) decreases ≥25%, including one subject with a
decrease of ≥50%. All decreases were transient and resolved while continuing on treatment.

7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, SAPPHIRE
There were no clinically significant liver biochemistry abnormalities in apitegromab-treated
(b) (6) , a
(b) (6) -old (b) (6) on
subjects noted in the MEP of SAPPHIRE (Table 33). One subject,
nusinersen randomized to receive 20 mg/kg of apitegromab, had a one time reading of alanine
aminotransferase (ALT) of 174 U/L (normal 5 to 45 U/L) on Day 29 associated with an aspartate
aminotransferase (AST) of 82 U/L (normal 9 to 50 U/L) and no concomitant increase in
bilirubin; this resolved by the next reading on Day 57 to 22 U/L and did not recur. (b) (6) had an
AE of gastritis a week prior, which may be a contributing factor. A causal role for apitegromab is
unlikely given the normalization of values while continuing on apitegromab.

There were no clinically significant abnormalities in liver biochemistry noted in subjects
(b) (6) , a
(b) (6) -old (b) (6) on
receiving apitegromab in the EXP (Table 34). One subject,
risdiplam, had persistent liver enzyme elevations during SAPPHIRE. At baseline, (b) (6) had mildly
elevated ALT (63 U/L, normal 10 to 33 U/L), and normal AST. (b) (6) continued to have high ALT
and mildly elevated AST throughout the study, with a peak ALT of 177 U/L on Day 57,
accompanied by mildly elevated AST (peak of 74 U/L on Day 57, normal 10 to 36 U/L).
Bilirubin remained normal throughout. (b) (6) did not discontinue apitegromab due to these
elevations and continued into ONYX (see Section 7.6.2.7). An abdominal echocardiogram
(b) (6)
demonstrated a cavernous hemangioma of the liver. The subject was also taking an
which may be involved in the development and growth of hepatic
hemangiomas (Conter and Longmire 1988; Glinkova et al. 2004; Evans et al. 2022). Liver
function test abnormalities are uncommon in patients with hepatic hemangiomas, but elevations
may occur (Maruyama et al. 2022). While a causal role for apitegromab cannot be fully ruled
out, given this subject’s underlying benign liver tumor, oral contraceptive use, and baseline ALT
elevation, it is unlikely these elevated transaminases are causally related to apitegromab.

7.6.1.8. Vital-Sign Analyses, SAPPHIRE
The Agency evaluated mean change from baseline for vital signs and incidence of TEAEs that
may be related to vital signs.
As seen in Figure 17 and Figure 18, there were no clinically significant differences between
apitegromab-treated subjects and placebo in height and weight over time in the MEP of
SAPPHIRE.

7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events

Results of the Agency’s analysis of the AEs for ONYX are shown in Table 38 below. A total of
180 subjects entered ONYX by the time of the original data cutoff of June 3, 2024. There were a
total of 138 subjects with AEs, including 23 subjects with SAEs. No AEs led to treatment
discontinuation. Most AEs were mild to moderate.

7.6.2.2. Deaths, ONYX
No deaths occurred during ONYX.
7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, ONYX
Table 39 shows SAEs observed during ONYX. Out of 180 subjects, 23 subjects (12.8%)
experienced SAEs, with the most common SAEs being in the system organ classes of Infections
(n=10, 5.6%), and Musculoskeletal Disorders (n=8, 4.4%). Overall, the SAE profile was
consistent with what was seen in SAPPHIRE.

7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, ONYX
Overall, there were no clinically significant laboratory abnormalities in chemistry, kidney
function, or hematology in subjects treated with apitegromab in ONYX other than CPK
elevations (discussed in Section 7.7.2).

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Increased Risk of Fractures
Issue
TEAEs related to fractures occurred at a greater frequency in the apitegromab dose groups
compared to placebo in the MEP in SAPPHIRE.
Background
Fractures occur frequently in the SMA population, due to low bone mineral density, muscle
weakness and low muscle mass, and decreased mobility, with estimated prevalences of 9.3% to
46% (Wasserman et al. 2017).
Myostatin, as an inhibitor of skeletal muscle growth, is thought to inhibit bone formation and
metabolism, and so drugs that inhibit myostatin or the myostatin signal pathway are theorized to
improve these aspects of bone health (Cui et al. 2023).
Assessment
Nonclinical
Adverse effects on bone were observed in toxicology studies conducted in rats. In a study in
which apitegromab (0, 10, 30, 100, or 300 mg/kg) was administered weekly by IV bolus
injection for 12 weeks, fissure at the femoral head physis was observed at all doses at the
terminal and recovery sacrifices. The fissure was associated with partial or complete absence of
the epiphysis and with degenerative (resorption) and reactive (fibrosis, woven bone, hyperplastic
osteoclasts) changes at the femoral neck and proximal metaphysis. Qualitative findings of partial
or complete missing femoral head epiphysis were observed in computed tomography images of
apitegromab-exposed animals. A no-effect dose for adverse bone effects was not determined for
this study. Weekly IV administration of apitegromab (0, 30, 100, or 300 mg/kg) to rats for 26
weeks, resulted in a dose-related increase in the incidence and severity of adverse changes in the
femoral head/neck (femoral head loss, thinning, fracture, fragmentation of the femoral neck)
observed by in vivo radiography at the end of dosing and recovery periods. A no-effect dose for
bone effects was not identified. The lowest effect doses for adverse effects on bone in the 12and 26-week rat studies (10 and 30 mg/kg, respectively) were associated with apitegromab
exposures (AUC) lower than that in humans at the MRHD. These nonclinical findings were
considered related to apitegromab.
Clinical
To further assess the discrepancy in fracture occurrence between apitegromab and placebo in
SAPPHIRE, the Division sent the following information request to the Applicant on June 30,
2025:
Provide narratives for all subjects with adverse events related to the term “fracture,”
including but not limited to foot, clavicle, femur, tibia, humerus, and torus fractures, for
both SRK-015-003 (SAPPHIRE) and SRK-015-004 (ONYX).

and 1 (2%) in placebo. All fractures occurred in the MEP (ages 2 to 12) and all events were
nonserious. All events had resolved by the 90-day safety update. In subjects treated with
apitegromab, time to first fracture ranged from 37 to 231 days from start of treatment.

Conclusion
The Agency notes that apitegromab may increase the risk of fractures in a population that is
already vulnerable to fractures due to their underlying disease. In SAPPHIRE, both apitegromab
dose groups had 8 subjects with fractures, compared to just one in placebo. Fractures continued
to be seen in ONYX, including serious femur fractures. While most subjects had potential risk
105
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
factors for fracture, many events did not have a clear precipitating event that led to the fracture.
Fractures occurred in both SAPPHIRE and ONYX despite most subjects being on either vitamin
D, calcium, or bisphosphonates (or a combination). Additionally, nonclinical studies are
suggestive of apitegromab-related adverse bone effects. The mechanism of action for this risk is
unknown. The prescribing information will include a Warnings and Precautions for fractures
based on the observed frequency of fracture events demonstrated in SAPPHIRE. The risk of
fracture can be significant in patients with SMA who may have other comorbidities (such as
osteopenia or a prior history of fractures) that may increase the risk of fractures. There is also a
concern regarding potential for more serious outcomes in the postmarketing period where
subjects are monitored less frequently than in a clinical study setting; therefore, a Warnings and
Precautions statement may mitigate potentially serious outcomes of fractures by alerting
providers to the risk. Additionally, based on the observed severity and potential seriousness of
the fracture events, enhanced pharmacovigilance for serious and nonserious fracture events will
be recommended in subjects with SMA treated with apitegromab.

7.7.2. Increases in Creatine Phosphokinase
Issue
Increases in serum levels of CPK were observed at a higher frequency and severity in subjects
treated with apitegromab compared to those in the placebo group in SAPPHIRE.
Background
Patients with SMA may have mild to moderate increases in CPK due to their underlying disease,
generally related to disease severity and muscle mass (Rudnik-Schoneborn et al. 1998). CPK
decreases from baseline have been noted in subjects treated with nusinersen (Freigang et al.
2021).Patients with SMA may have mild to moderate increases in CPK due to their underlying
disease, generally related to disease severity and muscle mass (Rudnik-Schoneborn et al. 1998).
CPK decreases from baseline have been noted in subjects treated with nusinersen (Freigang et al.
2021).
Assessment
Creatine Phosphokinase in SAPPHIRE
In the MEP of SAPPHIRE, CPK increases were seen more frequently in subjects treated with
apitegromab at either dose compared to placebo (Figure 23). Mean CPK levels increased by
91.0 U/L (10 mg/kg dose arm) and 45.3 U/L (20 mg/kg) by Month 12 compared to 3.0 U/L in
placebo. Twenty-four subjects (22.6%) treated with apitegromab overall experienced any
increase in CPK (9 in the 20 mg/kg group and 15 in the 10 mg/kg group), compared to six
subjects receiving placebo (12%). In the MEP, there were 21 subjects (21%) who shifted from
normal to any increase in CPK (9 in 20 mg/kg and 12 in 10 mg/kg) compared to six subjects in
placebo (14%). Additionally, while the majority of increases were mild, higher-grade increases
were seen only in apitegromab-treated subjects compared to placebo (Table 46).
