Conclusion
Elevations in CPK levels were observed at a greater frequency and severity in subjects treated
with apitegromab compared to placebo in SAPPHIRE. These events were nonserious and did not
lead to treatment discontinuations. Subjects were generally asymptomatic. The mechanism of
action and clinical significance of CPK increases in apitegromab-treated subjects compared to
placebo is unknown. Apitegromab, as an inhibitor of precursor forms of myostatin, may promote
muscle growth, but increases in CPK generally suggest increased muscle damage. While these
events of CPK increases were generally mild, nonserious, and did not lead to treatment
discontinuations, given the discrepancy in the frequency of CPK increases greater than 2.5×ULN
in apitegromab-treated subjects compared to placebo and the overall mean increase in CPK in
apitegromab-treated subjects compared to placebo, CPK increases will be included in Section 6
of the label of apitegromab under Laboratory Findings.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Hepatic and Renal Impairment
No clinical studies were conducted to evaluate the pharmacokinetics of apitegromab in patients
with renal or hepatic impairment. Apitegromab is degraded by proteolytic enzymes and is not
expected to undergo renal elimination or metabolism by hepatic enzymes.
Other Intrinsic Factors
Population pharmacokinetics analysis indicated that apitegromab PK was not affected by age,
sex, race, ethnicity, SMA type, GFR and albumin. The Applicant noted that no dose adjustments
are warranted based on these factors and the review team agrees with the recommendation.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Apitegromab is a human IgG4 monoclonal antibody (mAb) and is neither subject to metabolism
by cytochrome P450 (CYP450) enzymes nor expected to interfere with cytokine levels.
Therefore, no CYP450-mediated drug-interaction studies were conducted. Similarly,
apitegromab is not expected to be affected by transporters; therefore, no transporter-mediated
drug-interaction studies were conducted. Population PK analysis indicated that apitegromab
pharmacokinetics was not affected by use of background therapy – nusinersen or risdiplam.
Overall, no dose adjustment is warranted based on extrinsic factors.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
The indication for the draft labeling of apitegromab states that this product is for pediatric
patients 2 years of age and older with SMA. SAPPHIRE evaluated subjects between ages 2 to 21
with SMA. Based on the anticipated treatment response between pediatric and adult subjects, the
109
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
effectiveness of apitegromab has been established for the adjunctive treatment of SMA in
pediatric patients 2 years of age and older.
Apitegromab has orphan drug (March 22, 2018), rare pediatric disease (August 6, 2020) and fast
track designations (May 18, 2021). Because this product has an orphan indication, there are no
requirements under the Pediatric Research Equity Act to study pediatric patients.

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of
Reproductive Potential
There are no available data for use of apitegromab in pregnant individuals with SMA. No formal
studies of apitegromab in pregnant or lactating women have been performed. Pregnant or
lactating women were excluded from participation in apitegromab clinical studies in SMA, and
women of childbearing potential were required to use acceptable methods of birth control
throughout the study. There were no pregnancy events in SAPPHIRE, TOPAZ, or ONYX.

9. Product Quality
Although the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) review team has assessed BLA 761463
with respect to drug substance, drug product, microbiology and immunogenicity assays, and has
determined that it meets all of those applicable standards to support the identity, strength,
quality, and purity that it purports, the facility discipline has unresolved quality issues. During
(b) (4) drug product manufacturing
the inspection of the
facility for this application, investigators conveyed deficiencies to the representative of the
facility. The facility should provide satisfactory responses to these deficiencies to the FDA office
indicated on the FDA 483. Therefore, from an OPQ perspective, this BLA is not deemed ready
for approval in its present form until the above-mentioned issues are satisfactorily resolved. As
such, OPQ recommends a Complete Response (CR) action from a product quality perspective.

Considerations
Not applicable.

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
SRK-015 (apitegromab) was developed by Scholar Rock Inc. under IND 136872 for the
treatment of SMA in pediatric and adult patients 2 years of age and older who are currently on a
SMN targeted treatment. SRK-015 is a fully human recombinant monoclonal antibody.
On December 8, 2017, written responses were provided following an October 6, 2017, Type B
Pre-IND meeting request to discuss the development program for SRK-015. The Division
strongly recommended the Sponsor submit the protocol for the pivotal juvenile animal toxicity
study (with all supportive data) for feedback prior to study initiation.
Orphan drug designation was granted on March 22, 2018, for SRK-015 for the treatment of
SMA. The initial investigational new drug application (IND) was submitted on March 27, 2018,
along with a request for Fast Track Designation. The initial IND contained the first-in-human
phase 1 protocol SRK-015-001 entitled, “A Two Part, Phase 1, Double-Blind, Single and
Multiple Ascending-Dose Study to Assess Safety, Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics (PK) of
SRK-015 in Healthy Subjects.” The IND was allowed to proceed on April 25, 2018, and Fast
Track designation was denied on May 24, 2018. Rare pediatric disease designation was granted
on August 6, 2020.
On February 9, 2021, a Type C meeting was held to discuss interim data from a phase 2 study,
SRK-015-002 (TOPAZ) and proposed clinical plan for SRK-015 as an adjunctive treatment for
patients with SMA ≥2 years old. The Division stated Study 002 (TOPAZ) is not an adequate and
well controlled study, and therefore, does not permit interpretable assessments of efficacy or
safety of SRK-015 in the treatment of SMA to support a marketing application. The Division
stated the Sponsor would need to conduct one (or two, depending on the strength of the
evidence) adequate, well-controlled, add-on study(ies) that compare(s) subjects receiving SRK015 added to approved background therapy (e.g., nusinersen and/or risdiplam) to subjects
receiving approved therapy alone. The Sponsor acknowledged the Division’s feedback and
provided plans for a proposed adequate well-controlled phase 3 trial. In response to the
Sponsor’s Breakthrough Therapy Designation inquiry, the Division indicated that at this time
SRK-015 is not a strong candidate for Breakthrough Therapy Designation, which requires
preliminary clinical evidence that the drug demonstrates a substantial improvement on a
clinically significant endpoint(s) over available therapies.
On March 1, 2021, written responses were provided following a December 18, 2020, Type C
chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC)-only meeting request to discuss plans concerning
assay development and drug substance process validation. The Agency agreed with the proposed
(b) (4) , including SRK-015 in sequence
approach quantifying and characterizing
variants testing in comparability studies for significant process changes, and the overall process
performance qualification strategy for the manufacturing of SRK-015 drug substance.
On May 18, 2021, Fast Track designation was granted for the treatment of spinal muscular
atrophy in patients ≥2 years old.

On August 5, 2021, a Type B End-of-Phase 2 meeting was held to discuss results of the phase 2
study (TOPAZ) and the proposed phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial.
The Agency proposed patient populations (2 to 12 years as main efficacy population and 13 to 21
years as exploratory subpopulation), accepted the 12-month study duration and HFMSE total
score as the primary endpoint, and found the proposed stratification factors and statistical
approaches acceptable. The Agency recommended considering a 30 mg/kg dose level in addition
to the proposed 10 mg/kg and 20 mg/kg doses, noting that the correlation between latent
myostatin biomarker saturation and clinical efficacy is not firmly established.
On November 15, 2021, the sponsor submitted a phase 3 protocol, SRK-015-003 (SAPPHIRE)
(b) (4) . The Agency provided advice
which was intended to be the registrational trial for
on February 4, 2022, and advised the sponsor to maintain blinding in the open-label extension
study, record all clinically significant medical events as adverse events (even those potentially
related to disease progression), and provided several statistical recommendations.
On October 7, 2022, the sponsor submitted a phase 3 protocol, SRK-015-004 (ONYX) which
was an open-label, extension trial designed to evaluate the long-term safety and efficacy of
apitegromab in patients with Type 2 and Type 3 spinal muscular atrophy.
On August 4, 2023, the proprietary name, Isembyld, was found to be conditionally acceptable.
On September 24, 2024, a Type B CMC pre-BLA meeting was held to discuss product quality
and shipping validation requirements. On November 8, 2024, the sponsor submitted an expanded
access protocol SRK-015-008, titled “Expanded Access Protocol of Apitegromab for Patients
with Spinal Muscular Atrophy.”
On December 5, 2024, a Type B pre-BLA meeting was held to discuss the format and content of
the proposed marketing application for SRK-015. During this meeting the Agency agreed that
the proposed safety database appeared reasonable but requested additional blinded safety data

from the ongoing obesity study under IND
efficacy, the Agency acknowledged the Sponsor's plan to meet substantial evidence of

TOPAZ study and expressed significant concerns about the "low dose wins, high dose loses"
scenario where the 10 mg/kg dose was superior to placebo, but the 20 mg/kg dose was not,
noting this would be a major review issue.
Scholar Rock submitted BLA 761463 for SRK-015 on January 22, 2025. The Applicant

Priority Review and was exempted from the Pediatric Research Equity Act requirements due to
orphan designation for the treatment of spinal muscular atrophy.

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With
the Investigational New Drug Application
Pharmacology
In in vitro studies, apitegromab bound to pro- and latent myostatin from multiple species (mouse,
rat, rabbit, cynomolgus monkey, and human) with similar high affinity (8.09, 2, 2.5, 4.86, and
3.42nM, respectively), with no detectable binding to mature myostatin, pro- or mature GDF11
(protein with highest homology to myostatin), pro- or mature activin A, the mature forms of
BMP9, BMP10, or transforming growth factor beta 1. In vitro functional assays demonstrated
that apitegromab binding inhibited proteolytic activation of promyostatin and latent myostatin
with IC50 values of 580nM (mouse), 150nM (rat), 644nM (rabbit), 512nM (monkey), and 248nM
(human).
In vivo pharmacology studies conducted with apitegromab in severe combined
immunodeficiency (SCID) mice, demonstrated increases in total body lean mass (up to 17%) and
gastrocnemius muscle weight (up to 30%) at all doses tested (1, 3, 10, and 20 mg/kg/week
intraperitoneally (IP) for 4 weeks). In a mouse model of muscle atrophy, a dose of
20 mg/kg/week IP for 2 weeks fully protected mice from the atrophy-inducing effects of
dexamethasone. Inhibition of myostatin activation by muSRK-015P, the parenteral version of
apitegromab on a mouse IgG1 backbone, in combination with a small molecule SMN splice
modulator (SMN-C1), increased muscle size and function in a mouse model of SMA. In
Sprague-Dawley (SD) rats administered a single 10 mg/kg intravenous (IV) dose of apitegromab,
a 15.6% increase in gastrocnemius weight was observed at 28 days postdose.
Weekly IV administration of apitegromab (3, 10, or 30 mg/kg) to monkeys, resulted in increased
gastrocnemius and biceps brachii muscle weights (up to 25%) and increased thigh muscle
volume as assessed by magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) (up to 30%). Analysis of circulating
total latent myostatin, a marker of target engagement for apitegromab demonstrated a substantial
increase over baseline at all doses tested; however, the magnitude of increase was not dosedependent. While apitegromab binds to and engages both pro- and latent forms of myostatin with
similar affinity, only latent myostatin is detected in the serum.
Safety pharmacology assessments were incorporated into the good laboratory practice (GLP)
monkey toxicology study in which apitegromab (0, 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg/week) was
administered weekly by IV bolus for 4 weeks. No apitegromab-related effects on respiration rate,
neurological examinations, or ECGs were observed.
Pharmacokinetics
In female rats, administration of a single IV dose of apitegromab (10 mg/kg) resulted in
maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) and area under the concentration-time curve (AUC)inf
values of 203 µg/mL and 42936 µg*h/mL, respectively and a half-life (t1/2) of 222 hours. Repeatdose pharmacokinetics of apitegromab (3, 10, or 30 mg/kg) was assessed in male cynomolgus
monkeys administered weekly IV injections for 8 weeks, followed by a 4-week recovery period
(Study 8346509). The high dose (HD) was associated with Cmax and AUC values of 2648 µg/mL
114
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
and 1070638 µg*h/mL, respectively, and a t1/2 of 533 hours. Toxicokinetics (TK) was assessed
across multiple nonclinical studies in rat, rabbit, and monkey.
Because apitegromab is a mAb, studies to assess mass balance, metabolism, and excretion were
not performed.
Toxicology
General toxicology studies were conducted in Sprague-Dawley rat (durations of up to 26 weeks)
and cynomolgus monkey (4 weeks’ duration) using weekly IV administration. The formulation
used in the nonclinical studies (20mM histidine, 130mM sodium chloride, 0.02% polysorbate 80,
(b) (4) The HD was 100 mg/kg in the 4-week rat
pH 6.0)
and monkey studies and 300 mg/kg in the 12- and 26-week rat studies.
In the rat studies, findings included the expected effects of increased circulating latent myostatin,
increased body weight (BW), and increased skeletal muscle weights (biceps and gastrocnemius).
These were seen all doses in the 4-, 12-, and 26-week studies (10 to 300 mg/kg). In the 12- and
26-week studies, additional effects on bone were observed. In the 12-week study (0, 10, 30, 100,
or 300 mg/kg/week), fissure at the femoral head physis was observed at all doses, although not
with dose-related incidences, at the end of the dosing and recovery periods. The fissure was
associated with partial or complete absence of the epiphysis and with degenerative (resorption)
and reactive (fibrosis, woven bone, hyperplastic osteoclasts) changes at the femoral neck and
proximal metaphysis. No clear effects on bone mineral density (BMD) were observed; however,
qualitative findings of partial or complete missing femoral head epiphysis were noted in the CT
images only in treated animals and these animals were excluded from BMD analysis. Qualitative
findings of missing femoral head epiphysis were also seen in SRK-015-treated recovery animals.
These animals also showed bone loss at the proximal femur metaphysis, including at the femoral
neck region, from the qualitative evaluation of the CT images. The study report concluded that a
drug-related effect could not be ruled out. Bone findings in the subsequent 26-week rat study (as
assessed by bone radiography/densitometry) support an SRK-015-related effect on bone. Based
on these bone findings, a no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL) could not be identified for
this study. The lowest observed adverse effect level (LOAEL) (10 mg/kg) was associated with
Cmax and AUC values of 427 µg/mL and 46800 µg*h/mL, respectively (combined sex).
In the 26-week study in rat (0, 30, 100, or 300 mg/kg/week), changes in the femoral head/neck
were observed by in vivo radiography in animals from all groups, including controls, at the end
of the dosing and recovery periods. However, there was a clear dose-relationship in incidence
and severity of radiographic findings. The numbers of normal femur heads (score =0) at the
Week 27 evaluation were 48/60, 34/58, 10/60, and 0/60 in the C, LD, MD, and HD groups,
respectively. In a special histopathological examination of the femur, necrosis of the femoral
head was seen with no apparent dose-relationship or difference in the nature and severity of the
necrosis and associated fibrosis or bone/cartilage intraarticular debris at the terminal (1 C, 3 LD,
1 MD, and 1 HD males and in 1 HD female) and recovery (1 C, 1 LD, 1 MD, and 1 HD)
sacrifices. The femoral head findings were not considered to be drug-related, based on the
similar nature and severity of the presentation in all dose groups, including controls. However,
no explanation for these findings was given. According to the study pathologist, the lack of
correlating drug-related microscopic findings in the femur head/neck indicated that the
alterations observed radiographically were likely incidental, since histopathology is considered
more sensitive than radiography. However, this does not appear to be the case based on the dose115
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
related incidence and severity of the radiography findings. This and the previous findings in the
12-week study suggest an SRK-015-related effect on bone. Based on the bone findings a
NOAEL was not identified for this study. The LOAEL (30 mg/kg) was associated with Cmax and
AUC values of 1460 µg/mL and 126000 µg*h/mL, respectively (combined sex).
In the 4-week monkey study (0, 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg/week), circulating latent myostatin was
increased at all doses and muscle mass was slightly higher in SKR-015-treated groups but no
other drug-related effects were observed. This study included the safety pharmacology
assessments; no SRK-015-related changes in cardiovascular, neurological, or respiratory
parameters were observed. The NOAEL (100 mg/kg) was associated with Cmax and AUC values
of 5210 µg/mL and 577000 µg*h/mL, respectively.
Genetox and carcinogenicity studies were not conducted. The division waived the
carcinogenicity study requirement after a review of the Sponsor’s Weight of Evidence
justification (see review by Barbara Wilcox dated April 19, 2022).
A standard battery of reproductive and developmental toxicology studies was conducted. In the
fertility and early embryonic development toxicity study in rats, BW was increased in both sexes,
sperm concentrations were decreased (statistically significant [SS] at MD and HD), estrous cycle
length was increased, male and/or female mating and fertility indices were decreased, and
postimplantation loss was increased and live fetuses decreased at all doses. Although the group
differences were generally small and nondose-related and were therefore dismissed in the study
report, when taken together with the effects on sperm concentration and estrous cyclicity, the
reproductive effects appear to be drug-related. The LOAEL in the study was 30 mg/kg (no TK).
In the rat embryofetal developmental toxicity study, maternal BW gain and fetal BWs were
increased at all doses but no clear evidence of developmental or maternal toxicity was observed.
Mean postimplantation loss was increased at the LD and HD but was not SS because of high
variability across litters in those groups. The NOAEL (300 mg/kg) was associated with gestation
day (GD) 13 maternal Cmax and AUC values of 5940 µg/mL and 297000 µg*h/mL, respectively.
In the rabbit EFD study, there were three unexplained LD maternal deaths during dosing and LD
animals, one LD animal that was pregnant with no viable fetuses (all fetuses had been resorbed
early in gestation), and two LD animals that were not pregnant, leaving only 14 litters available
for evaluation. Fetal BW as decreased (SS) and postimplantation loss increased (13.5 versus
1.5% in C) in the LD group. All findings in the LD group were thought to be attributable to the
presence of endotoxin contamination in the dose formulation, but this was not clearly established
as a cause. Adequate number of litters were available at the MD and HD, and the HD was
considered a NOAEL in the study report (GD 14 AUC0-168 and Cmax were 1670000 h*ug/mL and
23800 µg/mL, respectively), but interpretation of the study is confounded by the possible
endotoxin contamination, particularly since many of the effects seen in studies of this mAb were
not dose-related.
In the rat pre- and postnatal development toxicity study, offspring BWs were decreased during
lactation and increased postweaning (all doses), sexual maturation was delayed (both sexes at all
doses), auditory startle response was dose-dependently increased (SS at MD and HD in
combined sexes), male mating and fertility indices were decreased (HD), and macroscopic liver
abnormalities (discolored, abnormally-shaped lobes) were observed (HD in both sexes). A noeffect dose for adverse effects on pre- and postnatal development was not identified. The
LOAEL was 30 mg/kg (no TK).

In a 7-week juvenile rat study in which IV dosing was initiated on PND 21, BWs were increased
in treated females at the end of the dosing (SS at all doses) and recovery (SS at MD and HD)
periods, latent myostatin and muscle weights were increased at the end of the treatment and
recovery periods (all doses in both sexes), and adverse reproductive effects were observed in
treated recovery females mated with naïve males. These consisted of decreases in corpora lutea
(SS at MD) with corresponding decreases in numbers of implantation sites (SS at LD and MD)
and increased (not statistically significant [NS]) preimplantation loss with corresponding
decreases in the number of live fetuses (values outside the historical control range at the MD and
HD). There were no apparent effects on BMD or bone histopathology. The LOAEL (30 mg/kg)
was associated with Cmax and AUC values of 1050 µg/mL and 106000 µg*h/mL, respectively.
A 3-week juvenile rat study with subcutaneous (SC) dosing from PNDs 7 to 28 was conducted to
support dosing of pediatric patients less than 2 years of age. There were no apparent effects on
juvenile development; however, the assessment of possible bone effects consisted only of femur
length measurement and routine histopathology. The NOAEL (275 mg/kg) was associated with
Cmax and AUC values of 2550 µg/mL and 382000 µg*h/mL, respectively, on PND 28.

13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted
With the New Drug Application
Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
In Vitro
Studies assessing apitegromab binding characteristics in the mouse (SRB015-17-01), rat (RPT00112), rabbit (RPT-00242), cynomolgus monkey, and human (SRB015-17-01) by biolayer
interferometry showed binding to promyostatin from all five species with affinities between 2
and 8nM (Table 48). The sequence of myostatin is at least 96% conserved among these species,
and the epitope for apitegromab within myostatin is 100% conserved among these species.
Assessment of binding to other closely related TGF-β family members indicated no detectable
binding to human mature myostatin, pro- or mature GDF11, pro- or mature activin A, or the
mature forms of BMP9, BMP10, or TGF-β1 (Table 49).

In primary pharmacology studies (SRP015-17-04, RPT-00120, RPT-00187, RPT-00112, RPT00242), recombinant pro- or latent myostatin was incubated with apitegromab and then subjected
to proteolysis by furin and/or tolloid proteases. Apitegromab bound and inhibited activation of
promyostatin from multiple species, with IC50 values between 150 and 644nM (Table 50) but
had no effect on the activation of the related growth factor GDF11. When the mechanism by
which apitegromab inhibits myostatin activation was investigated (SRP015-17-04), it was shown
that apitegromab inhibited generation of the tolloid-mediated cleavage fragment, indicating that
apitegromab functions via prevention of tolloid-dependent cleavage (Figure 24).

When muscle force was normalized to muscle weight, no effect of muSRK-015P was observed,

In a study in male SCID mice (8330236), a single IP dose of apitegromab (5 mg/kg) produced a

controls at 28 days postdose. PK parameters for apitegromab were: Cmax: 203 µg/mL, AUC∞:

Key Study Findings
Weekly IV bolus administration of SRK-015 (0 (vehicle) 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg/week) to SD rats
for 4 weeks (five total doses) resulted in increased BW and BW gain and increased circulating
latent myostatin at all doses. Clinical chemistry changes included non-dose-related increases in
CK in males. Drug-related anatomic pathology findings were limited to increased skeletal
muscle weights (biceps and gastrocnemius) at all doses without microscopic correlates. At the
NOAEL (100 mg/kg/week), Cmax values were 4500 and 4270 µg/mL and AUC0-168 values were
477000 and 428000 µg*h/mL in males and females, respectively, on Day 29.

Gross Pathology

Histopathology
At the terminal necropsy, mild to moderate fissure at the right femoral head physis was observed
in 17 rats, i.e., males at all doses (0, 3, 2, 4, and 1 in C, LD, LMD, HMD, and HD, respectively)
and 1 C, 2 LD, and 1 LMD females (Table 69). After recovery, the finding was observed only in
treated animals (1 LD and 1 HD males, 1 HMD female; Table 70). The incidence of physeal
fissure at the femoral head was not dose-related and for some animals the fissure was associated
with partial or complete absence of the epiphysis, and degenerative (resorption) and reactive
134
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
(fibrosis, woven bone, hyperplastic osteoclasts) changes at the femoral neck and proximal
metaphysis. A definitive cause of the fissure could not be identified. It was thought that the
handling conditions used for the originally intended CT evaluation may have compromised the
quality of the femurs for subsequent microscopic evaluation. But according to the study
pathologist, “in light of the presence of other changes in the metaphysis in 5 of 99 animals in the
main study and in 3 of 50 animals in the recovery study (resorption, fibrosis, increased woven
bone and/or osteoclast hyperplasia in the metaphysis), the physeal fissure was likely a real
antemortem change. In addition, based on what is known of femoral head necrosis in rats, and
given the higher incidence of physeal fissure at 10 and 100 mg/kg/week in males associated with
metaphyseal changes and absence of such changes in the control males, and absence of reactive
bone change in the one control female with physeal fissure, a treatment-related effect cannot be
ruled out.”

Source: Applicant submission

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

BLA 761463

Computed Tomography Imaging
No drug-related effects on total femur bone mineral density were observed at the end of dosing
and recovery. However, qualitative findings of partial or complete missing femoral head
epiphysis were noted in the CT images for 3 LD males, 2 LMD males and 1 LMD female, 2
HMD males, and 1 HD male. In addition, a “slipped out epiphysis of the femoral head which was
still intact on the posterior end” was observed in 1 HMD male. These animals were excluded
from BMD analysis. The LMD female had clinical observations of limited use of the right hind
leg on Day 86 of the dosing phase (day of scheduled sacrifice) and a macroscopic observation of
a fractured proximal right femur at necropsy. A CT image of the right femur from a LD male
with missing femoral head epiphysis showed bone loss at the proximal femur metaphysis, and 1
HMD male also showed bone loss at the femoral head epiphysis and proximal femur metaphysis
from the qualitative evaluation of the CT images. The HD male showed bone loss at the distal
metaphysis along with increased cortical porosity at the distal metaphysis and distal diaphysis.
The total femur BMD value for this animal was lower compared to other HD animals.
Missing femoral head epiphysis was also detected in some recovery phase animals (1 LD male, 1
HMD male, and 1 HD female). These 3 animals also showed bone loss at the proximal femur
metaphysis, including at the femoral neck region, based on the qualitative evaluation of the CT
images. The CT report stated: “Further investigation is needed to qualify this finding via
histopathology or similar techniques.”

Gross Pathology
No drug-related macroscopic findings were noted.
Histopathology
Drug-related microscopic findings were limited to hypertrophy of skeletal muscles. Minimal to
slight hypertrophy of muscles (biceps femoris, biceps brachii left and biceps brachii right) was
noted in both sexes at all doses. Hypertrophy was characterized by increased size of muscle
fibers compared to C. Minimal hypertrophy of muscle fibers persisted during the recovery phase
and occurred with a similar variability and lack of dose-relationship.
In the special histopathological examination of the femur, marked necrosis of the femoral head
was seen in 6/40 males (1 C, 3 LD, 1 MD, and 1 HD) and in 1/39 females (1 HD) at the end of
the dosing period, with no apparent dose-relationship or difference in the nature and severity of
the necrosis and associated fibrosis or bone/cartilage intraarticular debris (Table 79). Similar
findings were also observed at the end of the recovery period, with marked femoral head necrosis
observed in 4/20 males (1 C, 1 LD, 1 MD, and 1 HD) and in 1/20 females (HD), again with no
differences in either incidence or severity (Table 80). Therefore, the femoral head findings were
not considered to be drug-related, based on the similar nature and severity of the presentation in
all dose groups, including controls. The potential mechanism(s) underlying these bone findings
was not discussed.

Radiographic Imaging
Changes in the femoral head/neck were observed only by in vivo radiography in animals from all
groups, including controls, at the end of dosing and recovery (Table 81 and Table 82). However,
there was a clear dose-relationship in incidence and severity of radiographic findings, e.g., the
numbers of normal femur heads (score =0) at the Week 27 evaluation were 48/60, 34/58, 10/60,
and 0/60 in the C, LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. According to the study report, the lack
of correlating drug-related microscopic findings in the femur head/neck at either the dosing or
recovery phase necropsy indicated that the alterations observed via in vivo radiographic review
were likely incidental, since histopathology is considered more sensitive than radiography.
However, the sponsor has not provided an adequate basis for dismissing the radiography findings
considering the apparent drug-related increase in incidence.

Cesarean Section Data
At terminal sacrifice, 1 LD animal (B0115) was pregnant with no viable fetuses (all fetuses had
been resorbed early in gestation) and 2 other LD animals (B0106 and B0113) were not pregnant,
leaving only 14 litters available for evaluation. Although this was under the ICH recommended
16 litters for evaluation, since sufficient numbers of litters were available in the MD and HD
groups, the study could be considered adequate from that standpoint.
Fetal BW as decreased (SS) in LD males and postimplantation loss was increased in a non-dosedependent manner in treated groups (13.5 in LD versus 1.5% in C). Of the surviving LD animals
administered what was later determined to be endotoxin-contaminated formulation on GD 14
(Animals B0114, B0115, B0116, and B0119]), 3 were noted with partial or full litter resorptions.
However, postimplantation loss was increased for other LD animals administered

presence of endotoxin in the formulation administered on GD14. However, the shipment delay
makes that questionable (see Endotoxin Analysis below). All other C-section parameters were
comparable among groups.

Key Study Findings
Weekly IV (bolus) administration of SRK-015 (0 (vehicle), 30, 100, 300 mg/kg/day) to juvenile

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
Apitegromab is a human IgG4 mAb and is neither subject to metabolism by CYP450 enzymes
nor expected to interfere with cytokine levels. Therefore, no CYP450-mediated drug-interaction
studies were conducted. Similarly, apitegromab is not expected to be affected by transporters;
therefore, no transporter-mediated drug-interaction studies were conducted.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. Study SRK-015-001: Single- and Multiple-Dose
Pharmacokinetics and Pharmacodynamics in
Healthy Adult Subjects
Study Design
Study SRK-015-001 is a phase 1, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, sequentialcohort, 2-part study to evaluate the safety and tolerability of apitegromab in healthy adult
subjects. The study was conducted in 2 parts: Part A – single ascending dose (SAD) phase, and
Part B – multiple ascending dose (MAD) phase.
In Part A, a total of 40 subjects (20 to 55 years of age) were randomized to receive a single IV
infusion of 1 mg/kg (N=6), 3 mg/kg (N=6), 10 mg/kg (N=6), 20 mg/kg (N=6), or 30 mg/kg
(N=6) apitegromab or matching placebo (N=10). Blood samples for pharmacokinetics and latent
myostatin collected prior to infusion, at the end of infusion, and 4, 8, 24, and 48 hours
postinfusion at baseline, Days 1 and 2, and preinfusion on Days 7, 14, 21, 28, 42, 56, 84 and 112.
In Part B, a total of 26 subjects (18 to 54 years of age) were randomized to receive multiple Q2W
IV infusions of 10 mg/kg (N=8), 20 mg/kg (N=6), or 30 mg/kg (N=6) apitegromab or matching
placebo (N=6) for a total of 3 repeat doses. Blood samples for pharmacokinetics and latent
myostatin collected prior to the infusion, at the end of the infusion, and 4, 8, 24, and 48 hours
postinfusion at baseline, Days 1 and 2, and preinfusion on Days 7, 21, 35, 42, 56, 84, 112 and
140. The results from the SAD and MAD studies are summarized below (Table 138 and Table
139).

BLA 761463

Key Conclusions


Part A (SAD): Serum apitegromab exposures increased dose proportionally following single
IV infusions of apitegromab from 1 mg/kg to 30 mg/kg in healthy subjects. Median time to
maximum concentration (Tmax) across dose groups ranged from 2.02 to 6.00 hours after the
start of infusion. Mean CL values were consistent across dose groups (range: 0.145 to 0.185
L/day), and no dose-dependent trends were observed. Mean volume of distribution values
were consistent with blood volume (approximately 5 to 7 L). Mean t½ values were also
consistent across dose groups (range: 543 to 786 hours).



Part B (MAD): Serum apitegromab exposures increased in a dose-dependent manner
following multiple Q2W IV infusions of apitegromab from 10 mg/kg to 30 mg/kg in healthy
subjects. Median Tmax across dose groups ranged from 2.02 to 6.00 hours after the first dose
and from 2.02 to 37.99 hours after the third dose. Mean CL and Vz were similar among dose
groups in each treatment cycle. Mean accumulation ratio comparing exposure in the last
treatment cycle (Dose 3) versus the first ranged from 1.88 to 2.85 for AUC from time zero to
336 hours from start of infusion and from 1.42 to 2.12 for Cmax across dose groups.

The review team agrees with the Applicant’s PK analysis. The reviewer verified the Applicant’s
bioanalytical method validation and performance and considers it acceptable for quantification of
serum apitegromab concentrations in this study.

14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Performance
Apitegromab exposures in serum were determined using electrochemiluminescence assay. Test
method TM-TDI-0006 was used for analysis of samples from studies SRK-015-001 and SRK015-002. Briefly, the method employs blocked streptavidin-coated plates. A biotinylated
promyostatin was used to capture calibration standards and quality control samples prepared by
diluting stock solutions of SRK-015 into undiluted human serum to the desired SRK-015
concentration. Prepared calibrators, quality control samples and study human serum samples
were diluted to a minimum required dilution of 1:50 in assay buffer prior to loading onto the
precoated plates. The plates were incubated to allow the SRK-015 present in the samples to bind
to the target and subsequently washed to remove unbound material. A sulfo-tag ruthenylated
anti-human IgG4 antibody was then added to the plate to detect the captured apitegromab. The
plates were further incubated, and then washed to remove any unbound material. Meso Scale
Discovery (MSD) Read Buffer T was then added, and the plate was read using an MSD Sector
Imager. Upon electrochemical stimulation, ruthenylated conjugate on the antibody complexes
emits light on the electrode surfaces with intensity proportional to the concentration of free
apitegromab present in the sample. The method development and validation data are summarized
in tables below.
The method was then transferred and revalidated as Method IMA-ELAA-L004 and used for
analyzing serum samples from studies SRK-015-003 and SRK-015-004. This assay was reported
to employ identical protocol as TM-TDI-0006. The method development and validation are
summarized in tables below (Table 140, Table 141, and Table 142).

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

Isembyld (apitegromab)

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
conjugated via a long linker (SR8-C1-Fab-(PEG)12-biotin) as the capture antibody. The
interaction of SRK-015 and myostatin is pH dependent and at pH levels below 5.0, the
interaction is inhibited. Therefore, prepared calibrators, validation control samples, and study
human serum samples were diluted to a minimum required dilution of 1:30 using sample diluent
at pH 4.0 prior to loading onto the precoated plates. The plates were incubated to allow the latent
myostatin present in the samples to bind to the capture antibody, and pH 4.0 in the samples
results in dissociation of myostatin bound to apitegromab. The samples are subsequently washed
to remove unbound material. Ruthenium conjugated 71B9 is then added to the plate to bind the
latent myostatin bound to the capture antibody. The plates are further incubated, and then washed
to remove any unbound materials. MSD Read Buffer T is then added, and the plate is read using
an MSD Sector Imager. Upon electrochemical stimulation, the sulfo-tag (ruthenium) conjugate
on the antibody complexes emits light on the electrode surfaces on the plate. The method
development and validation data are summarized in tables below (Table 143 and Table 144).
The method was then transferred and revalidated as Method ELAA-00080RI-AV and used for
analyzing serum samples from studies SRK-015-003 and SRK-015-004. This assay was reported
to employ identical protocol as TM-TDI-007. The method development and validation data are
summarized in tables below (Table 143 and Table 144).
The Applicant also conducted cross-validation between the two methods for quantifying the total
latent myostatin concentrations in serum. However, there are concerns with interpretability of the
cross-validation data, which limits the ability to integrate the data across the two methods.
In response to an information request (Dated March 31, 2025), the Applicant indicated that they
measured only the latent myostatin concentrations in serum, but did not measure promyostatin
concentrations. They indicated that the conversion of promyostatin to mature, active myostatin
involves 2 catalytic steps: first a furin-like protease cleavage of promyostatin to latent myostatin
in skeletal muscle, and then a BMP1/tolloid-like metalloprotease cleavage to release the mature,
active myostatin. They noted that promyostatin is localized to skeletal muscle tissue and is not
detectable in serum, while latent myostatin is present in both skeletal muscle and serum. The
Applicant hypothesized that although apitegromab acts by binding to both pro- and latent-forms
of myostatin, it only inhibits the tolloid-like protease cleavage of latent to mature myostatin, but
not the cleavage from latent to mature myostatin.
In response to an information request dated July 3, 2025, the Applicant indicated that the Fab
version of SR8-C1 (capture reagent) binds to latent and mature myostatin, while 71B9 (detection
reagent) is selective towards pro- and latent myostatin only. Based on this combination of
selectivity of capture and detection reagents, the Applicant noted that only latent form of
myostatin contributes to the signal in the assay.

198

The review team agrees that the bioanalytical method validation and performance is adequate for
quantification of serum latent myostatin concentrations in studies SRK-015-002, SRK-015-003
and SRK-015-004 and are in accordance with the criteria for accuracy and precision as outlined
in the FDA guidance for industry Bioanalytical Method Validation Guidance for Industry (May
2018).
Overall, the serum latent myostatin concentrations measured in these studies are reliable, when
considered independently. However, there are concerns with the cross-validation data that limits
the ability to integrate data across trials. Specifically, the Applicant reported a bias of 0.49, and
noted the source of the difference between results in the two assays was due to lower impurities
in the more recent latent myostatin reference standard used in SRK-015-003 study. Based on this
information, the review team does not recommend integrating latent myostatin data across
studies in population pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics, or use of a “postanalytical correction
factor” as the Applicant proposed.
202
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
In up to 52 weeks of treatment in SAPPHIRE, 1/128 (0.8%) of subjects treated with apitegromab
tested positive for treatment-emergent anti-apitegromab antibodies (ADA) at a single time point.
No neutralizing antibodies were detected. However, because of the limited data regarding antiapitegromab antibodies, the effect of these antibodies on the pharmacokinetics,
pharmacodynamics, safety, and/or effectiveness of apitegromab products is unknown.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
Applicant submitted findings from population pharmacokinetic, PK/PD and E-R analyses. The
purpose of these analyses is to provide support for (A) mechanistic basis of apitegromab
treatment effects and (B) proposed labeling statements describing the effects of intrinsic/extrinsic
factors on apitegromab pharmacokinetics.
To provide mechanistic basis of apitegromab treatment effects, the Applicant


Characterized the relationship between apitegromab pharmacokinetics and total latent
myostatin pharmacodynamics in subjects with SMA using PD data collected in subjects with
SMA in Studies SRK-015-002 and SRK-015-003 and assess the impact of selected intrinsic
and extrinsic factors on the PK/PD relationships of apitegromab.



Characterized the impact of apitegromab or total latent myostatin levels on the clinical
endpoint, namely, HFMSE, using E-R modeling in subjects with SMA in Study SRK-015003.

The pharmacometric analyses (inclusive of population pharmacokinetics, population
pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics, and E-R analyses) were performed based on data
collected in healthy adult subjects in Study SRK-015-001, in ambulatory subjects with SMA
with or without SMN2 therapy and nonambulatory subjects with SMA with SMN2 therapy in
Study SRK-015-002, and in nonambulatory subjects with SMA aged 2 to 21 years old with
approved SMN2 therapy in Study SRK-015-003. The details of various studies included in the
pharmacometric analyses is shown in Table 145.

Population PK Analysis
The structural model was a 2-compartment model with linear elimination, maturation function on
CL, and time-varying WT on all clearance and volume parameters (parameterized as power
functions with fixed allometric exponents of 0.75 and 1, respectively). The effect of maturation
on the CL of apitegromab was included as a scaled function under the assumption that all
subjects had a gestational age of 40 weeks (i.e., full-term infants). Interindividual variability was
included on CL, Vc, and Vp. The interindividual variability on the Q was fixed to 10%. The
residual error model was proportional. A >10% mean relative change from baseline was
observed for age and weight for subjects with SMA, indicating the need to include time-varying
covariates in the analysis.
PK/PD Analysis
Figure 28 shows the outline of PK/PD model. A postanalytical correction factor was included to
address discrepancies in PD data between Studies SRK-015-002 and SRK-015-003. For more
details refer to Section 14.3.

205
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
Figure 28. Latent Myostatin PD Model

Source: Figure 4 on page 57 in smk-pmx-5702.pdf

Monte Carlo simulations were performed with the final PK and PK/PD model to evaluate
scenarios where dose delays were assumed to occur at steady-state conditions (i.e., 15 doses
were simulated) and a 1-, 2-, or 3-week delay as well as 1, 2, 3 or 4 missed doses were simulated.
E-R Analysis
An E-R analysis was performed for the primary efficacy endpoint (HFMSE) collected in Study
SRK-015-003. For the primary E-R analyses on efficacy endpoint, only the Main Efficacy
Population (i.e., 2 to 12 years old) was retained to develop the E-R model. An E-R analysis for
responders at the 12-month assessment (defined as a 3-point improvement in score of HFMSE
from baseline) was performed. The best treatment predictor (metrics of apitegromab and total
latent myostatin) were identified in the analysis.

Exposure-Efficacy Analysis
E-R analysis for efficacy was performed based on data collected in the Main Efficacy Population
in the pivotal Study SRK-015-003. Subjects were included in the analysis if they had valid
exposure values, change from baseline of HFMSE at Month 12, and no scoliosis surgery during
the study. A total of 145 subjects with SMA were included in the E-R analysis for efficacy;
subjects received placebo (n=45) or apitegromab 10 mg/kg (n=51) or 20 mg/kg (n=49)
administered IV Q4W in combination with SMN2 therapy (i.e., nusinersen or risdiplam). Timematched exposure metrics of apitegromab and/or total latent myostatin at Month 12 were derived
based on individual rich concentration-time profiles at the dosing cycle prior to the Month 12
assessments (Visit 14) simulated using empirical Bayesian estimates from the final PK and
PK/PD models and actual dosing history.

Montecarlo Simulations for Missed and Delayed Doses
Simulations were conducted to evaluate the impact of missed and delayed doses on the
pharmacokinetics of apitegromab and total myostatin levels.
PK and PD profiles following delayed doses administered 1, 2 and 3 weeks after the scheduled
time of the Q4W dose are shown in Figure 38. Exposure relative to steady state exposure
increases with increasing time of delayed dosing. However, the impact of delayed dosing on
apitegromab pharmacokinetics is minimal; relative to steady state, an approximately 2%, 6%,
and 9% increase in apitegromab median Cmax is expected for a delay in dosing of 1, 2, and 3
weeks, respectively.

219
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
Figure 38. Simulations of Delayed Dose Scenarios for Apitegromab and Total Latent Myostatin
Profiles for Apitegromab 10 mg/kg Q4W

Source: Figure 31 on page 131 in smk-pmx-5702.pdf

PK and total myostatin profiles with 1, 2, 3, and 4 consecutively missed doses of apitegromab
are shown in Figure 39. PK exposure following consecutive missed doses was significantly
reduced after returning to the regular planned dosing schedule; with a 25%, 38%, 44%, and 48%
decrease in apitegromab median minimum plasma concentration (Cmin) for 1, 2, 3 and 4
consecutively missed doses, respectively, compared to no missed doses. Total myostatin levels
(Cmin) were significantly reduced by 21%, 40%, and 60% for 2, 3 and 4 missed doses,
respectively, compared to no missed doses. To avoid potential loss of efficacy due to reduced
target engagement, it is recommended to resume a regular dosing schedule as soon as possible.

14.6. Pharmacogenetics
Not applicable.

15. Study/Trial Design
15.1. Study SRK-015-002, TOPAZ
TOPAZ was a phase 2, active-controlled study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of apitegromab
in subjects with later-onset SMA. It was conducted at 16 sites in the United States, Italy, the
Netherlands, and Spain. It ran from April 22, 2019 (first subject signed informed consent form)
and completed on February 28, 2024 (date the last subject completed the last study visit in the
48-month Study Period).
The primary objectives of the study were to:


Assess the safety and tolerability of apitegromab in subjects with Type 2 and Type 3 SMA
over 12 months



Assess the efficacy of apitegromab based on changes in motor function outcome measures
over 12 months

The primary endpoint was as follows:


Change from baseline in the Revised Hammersmith Scale (RHS) total score at Month 12 in
Cohort 1 and the change from baseline in the HFMSE total score at Month 12 in Cohorts 2
and 3.

15.1.1. Design, TOPAZ
The study design of TOPAZ is shown below in Figure 42.
Cohort 1 consisted of ambulatory subjects with Type 3 SMA, 5 to 21 years of age, who were
either NOT receiving nusinersen (apitegromab alone) OR who were already receiving an SMN2
therapy (initiated when the subject was 5 years of age or older) that they would continue to
receive in TOPAZ. Subjects were randomized to either 20 mg/kg apitegromab alone Q4W or
20 mg/kg apitegromab + nusinersen Q4W.

224
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5663512

BLA 761463
Isembyld (apitegromab)
Cohort 2 consisted of nonambulatory subjects with Type 2 or 3 SMA 5 to 21 years of age who
were already receiving nusinersen (initiated at age 5 or older). All subjects received 20 mg/kg of
apitegromab Q4W with nusinersen.
Cohort 3 consisted of nonambulatory subjects with Type 2 SMA aged 2 years and older already
receiving nusinersen (initiated when subject was less than 5 years of age). Subjects were
randomized to either 20 mg/kg apitegromab Q4W with nusinersen or 2 mg/kg apitegromab Q4W
with nusinersen.
Following completion of the primary 12-month treatment period, subjects had the option of
enrolling in three option, 52-week extension periods (A, B, and C), as shown below in Figure 43:

15.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, TOPAZ
The main eligibility criteria included males and females ages 2 to 21 years of age with a
documented diagnosis of 5q SMA Type 2 or Type 3 (ambulatory and nonambulatory) and a life
expectancy of >2 years. Nonambulatory subjects were required to be able to sit independently for
≥10 seconds and ambulatory subjects to be able to walk independently for >10 meters. Subjects
were also required to have scores of ≤62 on the RHS (for Cohort 1), or ≥10 on the HFMSE (for
Cohorts 2 and 3), and no further physical limitations that would limit the conduct of those
assessments during the study (such as severe scoliosis).

16. Efficacy
16.1. Results, TOPAZ
There were 65 subjects screened in the study, 58 of whom enrolled and were dosed with
apitegromab (23 subjects in Cohort 1, 15 subjects in Cohort 2, and 20 subjects in Cohort 3). A
total of 57 subjects completed the 12-month treatment period, and one subject treated with
apitegromab + nusinersen in Cohort 1 withdrew from the study during the 12-month treatment
period. Most subjects (94.8%) completed Extension Period A and enrolled in Extension Period
B; 87.9% completed Extension Period B and enrolled in Extension Period C; 84.5% completed
Extension Period C and enrolled in ONYX.
The majority of subjects enrolled in TOPAZ were white (72.4%) and ethnically not
Hispanic/Latino (91.4%). Thirty-one subjects (53.4%) were female. The mean age was 9.4 years.
The mean age at SMA diagnosis was 3.3 years (range: 1 to 16 years), and the mean age at
symptom onset was 2 years (range: 1 to 14 years). Half of the subjects had a history of scoliosis
(50%).
TOPAZ was ongoing during the COVID-19 pandemic, as such, for the primary efficacy analysis,
missing 3 consecutive doses due to site access restrictions caused by the pandemic was
considered an intercurrent event, and subjects with this event were censored and not included in
the 12-month analysis.
In Cohort 1, the primary efficacy endpoint was the mean change from baseline in the RHS total
score at Month 12. Cohort 1 demonstrated no change (stability) in their RHS total scores by
Month 12, regardless of whether subjects were on nusinersen (Table 150). In Cohorts 2 and 3,
the primary efficacy endpoint was the mean change from baseline in the HFMSE total score at
Month 12. Cohort 2 experienced a minimal mean score increase in the HFMSE by Month 12 (0.6
points), whereas in Cohort 3, subjects had larger increases in their mean HFMSE scores by
Month 12 regardless of whether they were receiving the active control dose of 2 mg/kg of
apitegromab or 20 mg/kg of apitegromab (Table 150).

The Applicant believes that if data is censored for subjects who underwent scoliosis surgery
during the extension periods of TOPAZ, then RHS scores remain stable in Cohort 1, HFMSE
scores remain stable in Cohort 2, and HFMSE scores improve over time in Cohort 3.
The data provided from TOPAZ suggests that apitegromab may support stabilization of motor
function over time, particularly in subjects with adjunctive SMN2 therapy, and especially in
younger subjects (Cohort 3, which enrolled subjects ages 2 and up, demonstrated the most
improvement at the 12-month mark and maintained stability at the 48-month mark, and had the
lowest mean age of all the cohorts (mean age of 4.0, compared to 12.6 in Cohort 1 and 11.7 in
Cohort 2)). However, there are several limitations to this study. TOPAZ was not a placebocontrolled study, and it involved multiple cohorts with a small number of subjects in each (and
each cohort had their own design and their own primary efficacy analysis). TOPAZ also enrolled
ambulatory subjects (these subjects were excluded in SAPPHIRE). Additionally, it is difficult to
determine, without a true placebo control, how much benefit subjects were getting from
adjunctive apitegromab, and not just from receiving nusinersen; this is especially true for Cohort
3, as subjects in this cohort had large improvements in their HFMSE at both the 20 mg/kg dose
and the “control” dose of 2 mg/kg of apitegromab. TOPAZ also only utilized subjects on
background nusinersen therapy in the original 12-month period, and not risdiplam, as in
SAPPHIRE (though three subjects switched to risdiplam during the extension periods). The
study also experienced limitations due to the COVID-19 pandemic. However, in conjunction
with the results of SAPPHIRE, the results from TOPAZ are supportive of improvements and/or
stabilization of motor function in patients with SMA who receive apitegromab as an adjunctive
treatment to SMN2 therapy.

18. Clinical Virology

19. Clinical Microbiology

20. Mechanism of Action/Drug Resistance
Apitegromab is a fully human immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4) monoclonal antibody that specifically
binds with high affinity to promyostatin and latent myostatin, which are inactive precursor forms
of myostatin. Apitegromab inhibits the tolloid-mediated cleavage of latent myostatin to it active
form, preventing activation and release of mature myostatin growth factor that inhibits muscle
growth.

21. Other Drug Development Considerations
Not applicable.

22. Data Integrity–Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other
Inspections)
Per the Office of Scientific Investigation report,7 Drs. Darras (site #101), Chiriboga (site #103),
Krueger (site #118), Mazurkiewicz-Beldzinska (site #601), Kotulska-Jozwiack (site #604), and
the Applicant were inspected in support of this BLA covering studies TOPAZ and SAPPHIRE.
Based on the inspection results, the studies appear to have been conducted adequately, and the
data generated by these sites and submitted by the Applicant appear acceptable in support of the
respective indication.

24. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
The following postmarketing requirement was required and agreed upon with the Applicant.
PMR 4891-1
Establish a worldwide single-arm pregnancy safety study to collect and analyze information for a
minimum of 10 years on pregnancy complications and birth outcomes for exposed pregnancies,
including women exposed to apitegromab during pregnancy and a relevant exposure period prior
to the start of pregnancy. Provide a complete protocol that includes details regarding how you
plan to encourage subjects and providers to report pregnancy exposures, measures to ensure
complete data capture regarding pregnancy outcomes and any adverse effects in offspring, and
plans for comprehensive data analysis.
